## VII The Key of the Parlour / 客厅的钥匙

===Original===
Elfrida was behind the secret panel, and the panel had shut with a spring. She had come there hoping to find the jewels that had been hidden two hundred years ago by Sir Edward Talbot, when he was pretending to be the Chevalier St. George. She had not had time even to look for the jewels before the panel closed, and now that she was alone in the dusty dark, with the door shut between her and the bright, light parlour where her brother was, the jewels hardly seemed to matter at all, and what did so dreadfully and very much matter was that closed panel. Edred had tried to open it, and he had fallen off the chair. Well, there had been plenty of time for him to get up again.

“Why don’t you open the door?” she called impatiently. And there was no answer. Behind that panel silence seemed a thousand times more silent than it ever had before. And it was so dark. And Edred had the matches in his pocket.

“Edred! Edred!” she called suddenly and very loud, “why don’t you open the door?”

And this time he answered.

“Because I can’t reach,” he said.

I feel that I ought to make that the end of the chapter, and leave you to wonder till the next how Elfrida got out, and how she liked the not getting out, which certainly looked as though it were going to last longer than anyone could possibly be expected to find pleasant.

But that would make the chapter too short﻿—and there are other reasons. So I will not disguise from you that when Elfrida put her hand to her pocket and felt something there﻿—something hard and heavy﻿—and remembered that she had put the key of the parlour there because it was such a nice safe place, where it couldn’t possibly be lost, she uttered what is known as a hollow groan.

===Chinese===
埃尔弗丽达正在暗板后面，那暗板装着弹簧，咔哒一声关上了。她到这儿来，本是想找两百年前爱德华·塔尔博特爵士（Sir Edward Talbot）假扮圣乔治骑士（the Chevalier St. George）那阵子藏下的珠宝。可暗板一关，她连找珠宝的工夫都没来得及有；如今她独自一人待在满是灰尘的黑暗里，一扇门把她和弟弟所在的那间明亮轻快的客厅隔开，珠宝好像压根儿不重要了，要命又顶顶要紧的，倒是那扇关死了的暗板。埃德雷德方才想把它打开，却从椅子上摔了下去。好在，他有的是时间再爬起来。

「你怎么不开门？」她不耐烦地喊。没有人应。在这暗板后面，寂静似乎比以往的任何寂静都要寂静一千倍。而且这么黑。而且火柴在埃德雷德口袋里。

「埃德雷德！埃德雷德！」她突然放开嗓门大喊，「你怎么不开门呀？」

这一回他答话了。

「因为我够不着。」他说。

我觉得，我真该就此收束这一章，让你们干等到下一章，去猜埃尔弗丽达是怎么出来的，去猜她喜不喜欢那份「出不来」——那滋味瞧着确实要延续好久，久得超出任何人所能觉得愉快的限度。

可那样这一章就太短了——况且还有别的缘故。所以我不瞒你们：埃尔弗丽达把手伸进口袋，摸到了一样东西——又硬又沉的一样东西——想起自己把客厅的钥匙放在了那儿，因为那是个顶妥当的安全地方，绝无丢失之理；这时候，她发出了一声所谓空空洞洞的呻吟。

===Original===
“Aha! you see now,” said Edred outside. “You see I’m not so stupid after all.”

Elfrida was thinking.

“I say,” she called through the panel, “it’s no use my standing here. I shall try to feel my way up to the secret chamber. I wish I could remember whether there’s a window there or not. If I were you I should just take a book and read till something happens. Mrs. Honeysett’s sure to come back some time.”

“I can’t hear half you say,” said Edred. “You do whiffle so.”

“Take a book!” shouted his sister. “Read! Mrs.﻿—Honeysett﻿—will﻿—come﻿—back﻿—some﻿—time.”

So Edred got down a book called *Red Cotton Nightcap Country*, which he thought looked interesting; but I don’t advise you to try it. And Elfrida, her heart beating rather heavily, put out her hands and felt her way along the passage to the stairs.

“It’s all very well,” she told herself, “the secret panel is there all right, like it was when I went into the past, but suppose the stairs are gone, or weren’t really ever there at all? Or suppose I walked straight into a wall or something? Or perhaps not a *wall*﻿—a *well*,” she suggested to herself with a sudden thrill of terror; and after that she felt very carefully with each foot in turn before she ventured to put it down in a fresh step.

===Chinese===
「啊哈！你这下明白了吧。」外面的埃德雷德说。「你瞧，我到底也不算那么蠢。」

埃尔弗丽达在动脑筋。

「喂，」她隔着暗板喊道，「我傻站在这儿也没用。我要摸索着上那个密室去。要能记得那儿有没有窗户就好了。我要是你，就取一本书来，读着等事情自己发生。哈尼塞特太太（Mrs. Honeysett）早晚会回来的。」

「你说的话我一半听不清。」埃德雷德说。「你说话就是含混。」

「拿——书！」他姐姐嚷道。「读！哈——尼——塞——特——太——太——早——晚——回——来。」

于是埃德雷德取下了一本叫《红棉睡帽之乡》（*Red Cotton Nightcap Country*）的书，他觉得看着挺有趣；可我不劝你们去读它。埃尔弗丽达呢，心咚咚跳得挺沉，伸出两只手，一路摸索着沿过道往楼梯那边去。

「都好说，」她对自己说，「暗板是好好在那儿的，跟我到过去那时候一样。可万一楼梯没了呢？或者压根儿从来就没有过呢？再万一我一头撞进一堵墙什么的里头呢？说不定还不是*墙*——是口*井*呢。」她对自己这么一提，猛地打了个寒噤；打那以后，每只脚都轮着先细细探过，她才敢往新的地方落下去。

===Original===
The boards were soft to tread on, as though they had been carpeted with velvet, and so were the stairs. For there *were* stairs, sure enough. She went up them very slowly and carefully, reaching her hands before her. And at last her hands came against something that seemed like a door. She stroked it gently, feeling for the latch, which she presently found. The door had not been opened for such a very long time that it was not at all inclined to open now. Elfrida had to shove with shoulder and knee, and with all the strength she had. The door gave way﻿—out of politeness, I should think, for Elfrida’s knee and shoulder and strength were all quite small﻿—and there was the room just as she had seen it when the Chevalier St. George stood in it bowing and smiling by the light of one candle in a silver candlestick. Only now Elfrida was alone, and the light was a sort of green twilight that came from a little window over the mantelpiece, that was hung outside with a thick curtain of ivy. If Elfrida had come out of the sunlight she would have called this a green darkness. But she had been so long in the dark that this shadowy dusk seemed quite light to her. All the same she made haste, when she had shut the door, to drag a chair in front of the fireplace and to get the window open. It opened inwards, and it did not want to open at all. But it, also, was polite enough to yield to her wishes, and when it had suddenly given way she reached out and broke the ivy-leaves off one by one, making more and more daylight in the secret room. She did not let the leaves fall outside, but on the hearthstone, “for,” said she, “we don’t want outside people to get to know all about the Ardens’ secret hiding-place. I’m glad I thought of that. I really *am* rather like a detective in a book.”

===Chinese===
脚下的地板踩上去软软的，仿佛铺了天鹅绒的地毯，楼梯也是一样。因为楼梯确实是在那儿的，错不了。她上得极慢、极小心，双手先在身前探着。最后，她的两只手碰到了一样像门的东西。她轻轻地抚摸，摸找门闩，不多时就摸着了。这扇门太久太久没开过，如今一点儿也不愿意开。埃尔弗丽达只好用肩膀顶、用膝盖撞，使出浑身的力气。门让步了——我看是出于客气，因为埃尔弗丽达的膝盖、肩膀和力气都小得很——屋里还是她见过的那副模样，圣乔治骑士就站在里面，就着银烛台上一支蜡烛的光，躬身微笑。只是如今埃尔弗丽达独自一人，光是一种绿蒙蒙的幽光，从壁炉台上方的小窗透进来，那窗户外面蒙着一幅厚厚的常春藤帘子。埃尔弗丽达若是刚从太阳地里来，准会说这是一种绿色的黑暗。可她在黑地里待了这么久，这片昏暝在她眼里已算相当亮了。尽管如此，她一关上门，还是赶紧拖了把椅子到壁炉前，爬上去开窗。窗是朝里开的，也一点儿不想开。可它也客气，肯顺着她的意思让步；窗子突然让开之后，她探出身去，把常春藤的叶子一叶一叶掰下来，密室里的白昼光便越来越亮。她不让叶子往外掉，都落在炉石上，「因为，」她说，「咱们可不想让外头的人把阿登家的秘密藏身处的底细全摸了去。幸好我想到了这一点。我实在*挺*像书里的侦探。」

===Original===
When all the leaves were plucked from the window’s square, and only the brown ivy boughs left, she turned back to the room. The furniture was all powdered heavily with dust, and what had made the floor so soft to walk upon was the thick carpet of dust that lay there. There was the table on which the Chevalier St. George﻿—no, Sir Edward Talbot﻿—had set the tray. There were the chairs, and there, sure enough, was the corner cupboard in which he had put the jewels. Elfrida got its door open with I don’t know what of mingled hopes and fears. It had three shelves, but the jewels were on none of them. In fact there was nothing on any of them. But on the inside of the door her hand, as she held it open, felt something rough. And when she looked it was a name carved, and when she swung the door well back so that the light fell full on it she saw that the name was “E. Talbot.” So then she knew that all she had seen in that room before must have really happened two hundred years before, and was not just a piece of magic Mouldiwarpiness.

She climbed up on the chair again and looked out through the little window. She could see nothing of the Castle walls﻿—only the distant shoulder of the downs and the path that cut across it towards the station. She would have liked to see a red figure or a violet one coming along that path. But there was no figure on it at all.

What do you usually do when you are shut up in a secret room, with no chance of getting out for hours? As for me, I always say poetry to myself. It is one of the uses of poetry﻿—one says it to oneself in distressing circumstances of that kind, or when one has to wait at railway stations, or when one cannot get to sleep at night. You will find poetry most useful for this purpose. So learn plenty of it, and be sure it is the best kind, because this is most useful as well as most agreeable.

Elfrida began with “Ruin seize thee, ruthless King!” but there were parts of that which she liked best when there were other people about﻿—so she stopped it, and began “Horatius and the Bridge.” This lasts a long time. Then came the “Favourite Cat drowned in a tub of Goldfish”﻿—and in the middle of that, quite suddenly, and I don’t know why, she thought of the Mouldiwarp.

===Chinese===
等到窗格子上的叶子全掰光，只剩下褐色的藤枝，她便回身来看这间屋子。家具上全都厚厚地覆着一层灰，方才脚下那么软，是因为地上铺着厚厚一层灰的地毯。那张桌子还在，圣乔治骑士——不，爱德华·塔尔博特爵士——在那上面搁过托盘。椅子都在，还有，千真万确，那座放珠宝的墙角柜。埃尔弗丽达不知怀着怎样一半是希望一半是恐惧的心情，把柜门打开了。柜里有三层搁板，珠宝却哪层都不在。其实哪层都空空如也。可是她扶着敞开的柜门时，手在门内侧摸到了一样粗糙的东西。她一看，是个刻着的名字；再把柜门整个甩开，让光正正照上去，她看见那名字是「E. 塔尔博特（Talbot）」。于是她明白了：先前在那间屋里见到的一切，必定是真在两百年前发生过，而不是一段穆迪沃普式的魔法。

她又爬上椅子，从那扇小窗望出去。城墙一点儿也看不见——只看得见远处白垩丘的坡肩，和横切过坡肩通向车站的那条小路。她倒真愿意看见一个穿红衣裳或紫罗兰色衣裳的人影沿那条路走来。可路上一个人影也没有。

你被关在一间密室里，好几个钟头没指望出去的时候，通常都做些什么呢？至于我，我总在心里念诗。这正是诗的用处之一——在这种愁苦的境地里，在不得不在火车站干等的时候，在夜里睡不着的时候，人就可以给自己念诗。你会发现诗在这方面大有用处。所以多多地背吧，而且务必挑最好的那种，因为最有用的也最悦人。

埃尔弗丽达起头念的是「毁灭降临于你，无情的王！」可那诗里有几段，她最爱有人在旁边听的时候念——于是她停了，改念〈贺雷修斯与桥〉。这首经念。接着又念〈心爱的猫淹死在一缸金鱼里〉——念到一半，十分突然地，我也不知是为什么，她想起了白鼹穆迪沃普（Mouldiwarp）。

===Original===
“We didn’t quite quarrel,” she told herself. “At least not really, truly quarrelling. I might try anyhow.”

So she set to work to make a piece of poetry to call up the Mouldiwarp with.

This was how, after a long time, the first piece came out﻿—

> “ ‘The Mouldiwarp of Arden
 

 By the nine gods it swore
 

 That Elfrida of Arden
 

 Should be shut up no more.
 

 By the nine gods it swore it
 

 And named a convenient time, no doubt,
 

 And bade its messengers ride forth
 

 East and West, South and North,
 

 To let Elfrida out.’ ”

===Chinese===
「我们不算真吵了架。」她对自己说。「至少不是真真正正地吵架。不管怎样，我总可以试试。」

于是她动手作一首诗，好用它把白鼹穆迪沃普召来。

过了好半天，头一首是这样出来的——

> 「『阿登的白鼹穆迪沃普，
> 凭着九位神明起誓：
> 阿登的埃尔弗丽达，
> 从此不再被关禁。
> 凭着九位神明起誓，
> 无疑也定下了个方便的时辰，
> 还差遣它的信使们出发，
> 向东向西，向南向北，
> 好放埃尔弗丽达出来。』」

===Original===
But when she said it aloud nothing happened. “I wonder,” said Elfrida, “whether it’s because we quarrelled, or because it just says he let me out and doesn’t ask him to, or because I had to say *El*frida, to make it sound right, or because it’s such dreadful nonsense. I’ll try again.”

She tried again. This time she got﻿—

> “ ‘Behind the secret panel’s lines
 

 The pensive *El*frida, reclines
 

 And wishes she was at home.
 

 At least I am at home, of course,
 

 But things are getting worse and worse.
 

 Dear Mole, come, come, come, come!’ ”

===Chinese===
可她大声念出来，什么也没有发生。「我琢磨，」埃尔弗丽达说，「是因为我们吵过架呢，还是因为它只是说他放了我出来、并没有去求他，还是因为我为了合辙押韵只好念成埃尔*弗丽*达，还是因为它简直胡扯得太不像话。我再试一回。」

她又试了一回。这一回她作出的是——

> 「『暗板的板缝后头，
> 幽思的埃尔弗丽达斜倚着愁，
> 但愿自己已经到家。
> 其实我当然是在家，
> 可事情越来越糟啦。
> 亲爱的鼹鼠，来、来、来、来吧！』」

===Original===
She said it aloud, and when she came to the last words there was the white Mouldiwarp sitting on the floor at her feet and looking up at her with eyes that blinked.

“You *are* good to come,” Elfrida said.

“Well, what do you want now?” said the Mole.

“I﻿—I ought to tell you that I oughtn’t to ask you to do anything, but I didn’t think you’d come if it really counted as a quarrel. It was only a little one, and we were both sorry quite directly.”

“You have a straightforward nature,” said the Mouldiwarp. “Well, well, I must say you’ve got yourself into a nice hole!”

“It would be a *very* nice hole,” said Elfrida eagerly, “if only the panel were open. I wouldn’t mind how long I stayed here then. That’s funny, isn’t it?”

“Yes,” said the Mole. “Well, if you hadn’t quarrelled I could get you into another time﻿—some time when the panel was open﻿—and you could just walk out. You shouldn’t quarrel. It makes everything different. It puts dust into the works. It stops the wheels of the clock.”

“The clock!” said Elfrida slowly. “Couldn’t that work backwards?”

“I don’t know what you mean,” said the Mole.

===Chinese===
她大声念了出来，念到末几个字时，白鼹穆迪沃普已经坐在她脚边的地板上，眨巴着眼睛仰头望她。

「你*真是*好心，肯来。」埃尔弗丽达说。

「好吧，这回你又要什么？」鼹鼠说。

「我——我该告诉你，我本不该求你做任何事。可我原想，要是真算吵了架，你就不来了。那不过是小小的一回，我们俩当场就都后悔了。」

「你这孩子性子直爽。」白鼹穆迪沃普说。「嗯，嗯，我得说，你把自己弄进了一个好窟窿！」

「要是暗板开着，这可是个*顶顶*好的窟窿。」埃尔弗丽达急切地说，「那我在里头待多久都不在乎。怪不怪？」

「是啊。」鼹鼠说。「唉，你们要是没吵过架，我本可以把你送进另一个时候——暗板开着的某个时候——你径直走出去就是了。你们不该吵架。一吵架，什么都变了样。它往机件里撒灰尘。它叫钟的轮子停摆。」

「钟！」埃尔弗丽达慢慢地说。「钟就不能倒着走吗？」

「我不懂你的意思。」鼹鼠说。

===Original===
“I don’t know that I quite know myself,” Elfrida explained; “but the daisy-clock. You sit on the second hand and there isn’t any time﻿—and yet there’s lots where you’re not sitting. If I could sit on the daisy-clock the time wouldn’t be anything before someone comes to let me out. But I can’t get to the daisy-clock, even if you’d make it for me. So *that’s* no good.”

“You are a very clever little girl,” said the Mouldiwarp, “and all the clocks in the world aren’t made of daisies. Move the tables and chairs back against the wall; we’ll see what we can do for you.”

While Elfrida was carrying out this order﻿—the white Mole stood on its hind feet and called out softly in a language she did not understand. Others understood it though, it seemed, for a white pigeon fluttered in through the window, and then another and another, till the room seemed full of circling wings and gentle cooings, and a shower of soft, white feathers fell like snow.

Then the Mole was silent, and one by one the white pigeons sailed back through the window into the blue and gold world of out-of-doors.

“Get up on a chair and keep out of the way,” said the Mouldiwarp. And Elfrida did.

And then a soft wind blew through the little room﻿—a wind like the wind that breathes softly in walled gardens and shakes down the rose-leaves on sparkling summer mornings. And the white feathers on the floor were stirred by the sweet wind, and drifted into little heaps and lines and curves till they made on the dusty floor the circle of a clock-face, with all its figures and its long hand and its short hand and its second hand. And the white Mole stood in the middle.

===Chinese===
「我自己也说不清是不是真懂。」埃尔弗丽达解释道，「是那个雏菊钟。你坐在秒针上，时间就没有了——可你没坐着的地方，时间还多得很。我要能坐上雏菊钟，那么在有人来放我出去之前，时间就什么都不是了。可我够不着那雏菊钟，哪怕你肯给我变一个。所以*那*没用。」

「你是个非常聪明的小姑娘。」白鼹穆迪沃普说，「再说，天下的钟也不是全拿雏菊做的。把桌子椅子挪回去靠墙；我们来看看能为你做点什么。」

埃尔弗丽达照办的时候——白鼹鼠用后腿站着，用一种她听不懂的语言轻声唤起来。不过别的显然听得懂，因为一只白鸽扑棱着从窗口飞进来，接着又是一只又一只，直到满屋子仿佛都是盘旋的翅膀和咕咕的轻鸣，一阵柔软的白羽毛像雪似的落下来。

随后鼹鼠不响了，白鸽一只接一只地翩然飞回窗外，回到外面那个蓝金两色的世界里去。

「站到椅子上去，别碍事。」白鼹穆迪沃普说。埃尔弗丽达照办了。

于是一阵柔风吹过了这间小屋——像围墙花园里轻轻呼吸的风，像灿烂的夏日清晨摇落玫瑰花瓣的风。地板上的白羽毛被这香甜的风搅动，飘成一小堆一小堆、一条线一条线、一道弯一道弯，最后在积灰的地板上拼成一个钟面的圆圈，数字齐全，长针、短针、秒针样样俱全。白鼹鼠就站在正当中。

===Original===
“All white things obey me,” it said. “Come, sit down on the minute hand, and you’ll be there in no time.”

“Where?” asked Elfrida, getting off the chair.

“Why, at the time when they open the panel. Let me get out of the clock first. And give me the key of the parlour door. It’ll save time in the end.”

So Elfrida sat down on the minute hand, and instantly it began to move round﻿—faster than you can possibly imagine. And it was very soft to sit on﻿—like a cloud would be if the laws of nature ever permitted you to sit on clouds. And it spun round so that it seemed no time at all before she found herself sitting on the floor and heard voices, and knew that the secret panel was open.

“I see,” she said wisely, “it does work backwards, doesn’t it?”

But there was no one to answer her, for the Mouldiwarp was gone. And the white pigeons’ feathers were in heaps on the floor. She saw them, as she stood up. And there wasn’t any clock-face any more.

===Chinese===
「天下的白色东西都听我的。」它说。「来，坐到分针上去，一眨眼你就到啦。」

「到哪儿？」埃尔弗丽达问着，从椅子上下来。

「嗐，到他们打开暗板的那个时刻呀。先让我从钟里出去。还有，把客厅门的钥匙给我。到头来能省时间。」

于是埃尔弗丽达在分针上坐下来，分针立刻转了起来——快得你根本没法想象。坐在上面软软的——倘若自然法则容许你坐到云上去，云大概就是这个样子。它转啊转啊，她仿佛转眼之间就发觉自己坐在了地板上，听见了人声，知道暗板已经开了。

「我明白了。」她一脸高明地说，「它果真能倒着走，对吧？」

可是没有人回答她，因为白鼹穆迪沃普已经走了。白鸽的羽毛一堆一堆地落在地板上。她站起来的时候看见了。钟面也不复存在了。

---

===Original===
Edred soon got tired of *Red Cotton Nightcap Country*, which really is not half such good fun as it sounds, even for grownups, and he tried several other books. But reading did not seem amusing, somehow. And the house was so much too quiet, and the clock outside ticked so much too loud﻿—and Elfrida was shut up, and there were bars to the windows, and the door was locked. He walked about, and sat in each of the chairs in turn, but no one of them was comfortable. And his thoughts were not comfortable either. Suppose no one ever came to let them out! Supposing the years rolled on and found him still a prisoner, when he was a white-haired old man, like people in the Bastille, or in Iron Masks? His eyes filled with tears at the thought. Fortunately it did not occur to him that unless someone came pretty soon he would be unlikely to live to a great age, since people cannot live long without eating. If he had thought of this he would have been even more unhappy than he was﻿—and he was quite unhappy enough. Then he began to wonder if “anything had happened” to Elfrida. She was dreadfully quiet inside there behind the panel. He wished he had not quarrelled with her. Everything was very miserable. He went to the window and looked out, as Elfrida had done, to see if he could see a red dress or a violet dress coming over the downs. But there was nothing. And the time got longer and longer, drawing itself out like a putty snake, when you rub it between your warm hands﻿—and at last, what with misery, and having cried a good deal, and its being long past teatime, he fell asleep on the window-seat.

He was roused by a hand on his shoulder and a voice calling his name.

Next moment he was in the arms of Aunt Edith, or as much in her arms as he could be with the window-bars between them.

When he had told her where Elfrida was, and where the room-key was, which took some time, he began to cry again﻿—for he did not quite see, even now, how he was to be got out.

===Chinese===
埃德雷德很快就腻了那本《红棉睡帽之乡》——这书实在不像书名那么好玩，连大人玩着读也没意思——他又换了几本别的书。可是不知怎么，读书也不来劲。屋子静得太过了，外面的钟滴答得又太响——埃尔弗丽达还关在里头，窗户上装着铁条，门也锁着。他走来走去，挨着把每把椅子都坐了一遍，没有一把坐得舒服。他的心事也不舒服。万一永远没有人来放他们出去呢！万一一年一年滚过去，等他成了白头发的老头儿还关在这儿，像巴士底狱里的人，像戴铁面罩的人一样呢？想到这儿，他眼睛里满是泪水。幸亏他没想到：要是不赶紧有人来，他多半活不到很大的年纪，因为人不吃饭是活不了多久的。他若是想到了这个，就会比现在更难过——而他现在已经够难过的了。接着他又开始担心埃尔弗丽达是不是「出了什么事」。她在暗板后头静得吓人。他后悔不该跟她吵架。样样事情都惨兮兮的。他走到窗前，像埃尔弗丽达那样往外望，看能不能望见一件红衣裳或一件紫罗兰色的衣裳翻过白垩丘来。可是什么也没有。时间越拖越长，像一条油灰搓的蛇，在你温暖的两个手掌间越搓越长——到末了，又是心里难受，又是哭了一大场，又早过了吃茶点的时候，他就在窗座上睡着了。

一只手搭上他的肩膀，一个声音唤着他的名字，把他惊醒了。

转眼间他已投进伊迪丝姑姑的怀里——或者说，隔着窗上的铁条，能搂多近就搂多近地投进了她的怀里。

他告诉了她埃尔弗丽达在哪儿、房门钥匙又在哪儿——这费了些工夫——说完他又哭起来，因为即便是现在，他也想不明白自己怎么才能出去。

===Original===
“Now don’t be a dear silly,” said Aunt Edith. “If we can’t get you out any other way I’ll run and fetch a locksmith. But look what I found right in the middle of the path as I came up from the station.”

It was a key. And tied to it was an ivory label, and on the label were written the words, “Parlour door, Arden.”

“You might try it,” she said.

He did try it. And it fitted. And he unlocked the parlour door and then the front door, so that Aunt Edith could come in.

And together they got the kitchen steps and found the secret spring and opened the panel, and got out the dusty Elfrida. And then Aunt Edith lighted the kitchen fire and boiled the kettle; they had tea, which everyone wanted very badly indeed. And Aunt Edith had brought little cakes for tea with pink icing on them, very soft inside with apricot jam. And she had come to stay over Sunday.

She was as much excited as the children over the secret panel, and after tea (when Edred had fetched Emily back from the wild-goose chase for a parcel at the station, on which she was still engaged), the aunt and the niece and the nephew explored the secret stair and the secret chamber thoroughly.

“What a wonderful lot of pigeons’ feathers!” said Aunt Edith; “they must have been piling up here for years and years.”

“It was lucky, you finding that key,” said Edred. “I wonder who dropped it. Where’s the other one, Elf?”

“I don’t know,” said Elfrida truthfully, “it isn’t in my pocket now.”

And though Edred and Aunt Edith searched every corner of the secret hiding-place they never found that key.

===Chinese===
「好了，别犯傻啦，小傻瓜。」伊迪丝姑姑说。「要是想不出别的法子弄你出来，我就跑去喊个锁匠来。可是你瞧，我从车站上来的时候，在小路正当间儿捡到了什么。」

那是一把钥匙。钥匙上拴着一张象牙牌子，牌子上写着几个字：「客厅门，阿登。」

「你可以试试。」她说。

他试了。正合适。他开了客厅的门，又开了前门，好让伊迪丝姑姑进来。

然后他们俩搬来厨房的梯凳，找到了暗簧，打开暗板，把满身灰尘的埃尔弗丽达弄了出来。接着伊迪丝姑姑生了厨房的火，烧上水壶；他们吃了茶点——人人都实在饿坏了。伊迪丝姑姑还带来了茶点吃的小蛋糕，上面挂着粉红的糖霜，里头软软的，夹着杏子酱。她是来住过星期日的。

她同孩子们一样，对那暗板兴奋得很。吃过茶点（这时候埃德雷德已把艾米丽（Emily）从车站那趟白跑的差事上叫了回来——她还在那儿没头没脑地找那个包裹呢），姑母带着侄女侄子，把秘密楼梯和密室彻底探了一遍。

「好多的鸽子毛呀，真了不起！」伊迪丝姑姑说，「准是一年一年堆积下来的。」

「您捡着那把钥匙，真是运气。」埃德雷德说。「不知是谁掉的。另一把呢，埃尔夫？」

「不知道。」埃尔弗丽达老老实实地说，「反正不在我口袋里了。」

后来埃德雷德和伊迪丝姑姑把秘密藏身处的每个角落都搜遍了，也始终没找着那把钥匙。

===Original===
Elfrida alone knows that she gave it to the Mouldiwarp. And as Mrs. Honeysett declared that there had never been a parlour key with a label on it in *her* time it certainly does seem as though the Mole must have put the key he got from Elfrida on the path for Aunt Edith to find, after carefully labelling it to prevent mistakes. How the Mole got the label is another question, but I really think that finding a label for a key is quite a simple thing to do﻿—I have done it myself. Whereas making a clock-face of white pigeon feathers is very difficult indeed﻿—and a thing that I have never been able to do. And as for making that clock-face the means of persuading time to go fast or slow, just as one wishes﻿—well, I don’t suppose even *you* could do that.

Elfrida found it rather a relief to go back to the ordinary world, where magic moles did not upset the clock﻿—a world made pleasant by nice aunts and the old delightful games that delight ordinary people. Games such as “Hunt the thimble,” “What is my thought like,” and “Proverbs.” The three had a delightful weekend, and Aunt Edith told them all about the lodgers and the seaside house, which already seemed very long ago and far away. On Sunday evening, as they walked home from Arden Church, where they had tried to attend to the service, and not to look *too* much at the tombs and monuments of dead-and-gone Ardens that lined the chancel, the three sat down on Arden Knoll, and Aunt Edith explained things a little to them. She told them much more than they could understand about wills, and trustees, and incomes, but they were honoured by her confidence, and pleased by the fact that she seemed to think they *could* understand such grown-up kind of things. And the thing that remained on their minds after the talk, like a ship cast up by a high tide, was this: that Arden Castle was theirs, and that there was very little money to “keep it up” with. So that everyone must be very careful, and no one must be at all extravagant. And Aunt Edith was going back to the world of lawyers, and wills, and trustees, early on Monday morning, and they must be very good children, and not bother Mrs. Honeysett, and never, never lock themselves in and hide the key in safe places.

===Chinese===
只有埃尔弗丽达一个人知道，钥匙是她给了白鼹穆迪沃普的。而哈尼塞特太太一口咬定，*她*那个年头从来没有过一把拴牌子的客厅钥匙；这么看来，那多半是鼹鼠把它从埃尔弗丽达手里得来的钥匙，仔细系上牌子、免得出错之后，搁在小路上让伊迪丝姑姑捡的。鼹鼠从哪儿弄来牌子是另一个问题，不过我真心觉得，给钥匙找块牌子是顶容易的事——我自己就做过。可是拿白鸽的羽毛做一个钟面，那可真是难极了——是我一辈子也做不来的。至于让那钟面成为说服时间快走慢走、全随人愿的手段——嗐，我看连*你*也办不到。

埃尔弗丽达觉得回到寻常世界来倒是松了口气，在那里，魔法鼹鼠不会把钟拨乱——那是个由和气的姑姑和逗乐普通人开心的老游戏装点得舒舒服服的世界。诸如「找顶针」（Hunt the thimble）、「我的心思像什么」（What is my thought like）、「谚语」（Proverbs）之类的游戏。他们三个过了一个快活的周末，伊迪丝姑姑把房客们和海边房子的种种讲给他们听，那些事仿佛已经很远很久了。星期日傍晚，他们从阿登教堂走回家去——在教堂里他们本想好好听道，别*过分*去看圣坛两侧排着的、死去的阿登家先人们的坟墓和碑碣——随后三人在阿登丘上坐下，伊迪丝姑姑把事情给他们稍稍讲了一点。她讲的遗嘱啦、受托人啦、进项啦，远超他们能听懂的范围，可他们被她的信任感动得很光彩，又因为她似乎认为他们*能*懂这类大人的事情而欢喜。谈过之后留在他们心上的东西，就像大潮冲上岸的一条船，是这样一件事：阿登城堡归他们了，而用来「维持」它的钱少得很。所以人人必须非常节省，谁也不许有半点铺张。星期一一大早，伊迪丝姑姑就要回律师、遗嘱和受托人的世界里去，他们要做很乖的孩子，不去烦扰哈尼塞特太太，而且永远、永远不再把自己锁在里面，再把钥匙藏到安全的地方去。

===Original===
All this remained, as the lasting result of the pleasant talk on the downs in the softly lessening light.

And another thing remained, which Edred put into words as the two children walked back from the station, where they had seen Aunt Edith into the train and waved their goodbyes to her.

“It is very important indeed,” he said, “for us to find the treasure. Then we could ‘keep up’ the Castle without any bother. We must have it *built* up again first, of course, and then we’ll *keep* it up. And we won’t have any old clocks and not keeping together, this time. We’ll both of us go and find the attic the minute our quarrel’s three days old, and we’ll ask the Mouldiwarp to send us to a time when we can really *see* the treasure with our own eyes. I do think that’s a good idea, don’t you?” he asked, with modest pride.

“Very,” Elfrida said. “And I say, Edred, I don’t mean to quarrel any more if I can help it. It is such waste of time,” she added in her best grown-up manner, “and it does delay everything so. Delays are dangerous. It says so in the ‘proverb’ game. Suppose there really was a chance of *getting* the treasure and we had to wait three days because of quarrelling. But I’ll tell you one thing I found out: you can get the Mole to come and help you, even if you have quarrelled a little. Because *I* did.” And she told him how.

“But, I expect,” she added. “It would only come if I were in the most awful trouble and all human aid despaired of.”

“Well, we’re not that now,” said Edred, knocking the head off a poppy with his stick, “and I’m jolly glad we’re not.”

===Chinese===
在丘岗上渐弱渐柔的天光里那场愉快的谈话，留下了这一切，经久不散。

留下的还有一件事，是两个孩子从车站往回走时，由埃德雷德说出口的——他们刚在车站把伊迪丝姑姑送上了火车，向她挥手道了别。

「咱们找到宝藏，实在是要紧得很。」他说。「那样咱们就能毫无烦难地『维持』城堡了。当然，得先把它重新*建*起来，然后咱们再*维持*它。这一回，咱们不要再搞什么旧钟啊、不齐心啊。咱们俩一等到吵架满三天，就立刻上阁楼去，求白鼹穆迪沃普把咱们送到一个能真正亲眼*见到*宝藏的时代去。我当真觉得这是个好主意，你说是不是？」他问道，带着一点谦虚的得意。

「很好。」埃尔弗丽达说。「还有，埃德雷德，我说，只要做得到，我再也不吵架了。那太浪费时间了。」她用她最像大人的腔调补了一句，「而且事事都给耽误了。耽误是危险的。『谚语』游戏里就是这么说的。万一真有个*捞到*宝藏的机会，却因为吵架非等三天不可呢。不过我要告诉你一件我发现了的事：哪怕你们小小地吵过一架，你也照样能叫鼹鼠来帮忙。因为*我*就叫来过。」于是她把经过讲给他听。

「不过我料想，」她补了一句，「得是我陷入天大的困境、一切人力都不济事的时候，它才肯来。」

「反正咱们现在不是那样。」埃德雷德说着，用手杖敲掉了一朵罂粟花的头，「咱们不是那样，我高兴得很。」

===Original===
“I wonder,” said Elfrida, “who lives in that cottage where the witch was. I know exactly where it is. I expect it’s been pulled down, though. Let’s go round that way. It’ll be something to do.”

So they went round that way, and the way was quite easy to find. But when they got to the place where the tumbledown cottage had been in Boney’s time, there was only a little slate-roofed house with a blue bill pasted up on its yellow-brick face saying that somebody’s A1 ginger-beer and up-to-date minerals were sold there. The house was dull to look at, and they did not happen to have any spare money for ginger-beer, so they turned round to go home and suddenly found themselves face to face with a woman. She wore a red-and-black plaid blouse and a bought ready-made black skirt, and on her head was a man’s peaked cap such as women in the country wear now instead of the pretty sun bonnets that they used to wear when I was a little girl.

“So they’ve pulled the old cottage down,” she said. “This new house’ll be fine and dry inside, I lay. The rain comes in through the roof of the old one so’s you might a’most as well be laying in the open medder.”

The children listened politely, and both were wondering where they had seen this woman before, for her face was strangely familiar to them, and yet they didn’t seem really to know her either.

“Most of the cottages ’bout here is just as bad as they always was,” she went on. “When Arden has the handling of the treasure he’ll see to it that poor folks lie warm and dry, won’t he now?”

===Chinese===
「不知那座巫婆待过的小屋，如今谁住着。」埃尔弗丽达说。「那地方在哪儿我一清二楚。不过我料想早拆掉了。咱们绕那边走一趟吧。也算件事做。」

于是他们绕了那条道，路很好找。可是到了波尼（Boney）年代那座塌塌的小屋原在的地方，只见一所石板瓦顶的小房子，黄砖墙面上贴着一张蓝招贴，说这儿出售某家的甲级姜汁啤酒和最新式的汽水。那房子看着乏味，他们身上又偏偏没有买姜汁啤酒的闲钱，于是转身往家走，忽然同一个女人打了个照面。她穿一件红黑格子的法兰绒短衫，一条买来的现成黑裙子，头上戴一顶男人的鸭舌帽——如今乡下女人都兴戴这个，不戴我小时候她们戴的那种好看的遮阳软帽了。

「这么说，老屋子是拆掉啦。」她说。「这新房子里头保准又好又干燥，我敢说。老房子的雨从屋顶里漏下来，漏得你躺在里头，差不多跟躺在野地里一样。」

孩子们客客气气地听着，俩人都在纳闷从前在哪儿见过这个女人，因为那张脸他们奇怪地眼熟，可又好像并不真认识她。

「这一带的小屋，多半还是跟从前一样糟。」她接着说。「等阿登家得了宝藏的手，他管保会叫穷人们睡得又暖和又干燥，是不是？」

===Original===
And then all in a minute the children both knew, and she knew that they knew.

“Why,” said Edred, “you’re the﻿—”

“Yes,” she said, “I’m the witch come from old ancient times. If you can go back I can go forth, because then and now’s the same if I know how to make a clock.”

“Can you make clocks?” said Elfrida. “I thought it was only﻿—”

“So it be,” said the witch. “I can’t make ’em, but I know them as can. And I’ve come ’ere to find you, ’cause you brought me the tea and sugar. I’ve got the wise eye, I have. I can see back and forth. I looked forrard and I saw ye, and I looked back and I saw what you’re seeking, and I know where the treasure is and﻿—”

“But where did you get those clothes?” Edred asked; and it was a question he was afterwards to have reason to regret.

“Oh, clothes is easy come by,” said the witch. “If it was only clothes I could be a crowned queen this very minute.”

The children had a fleeting impression of seeing against the crisscross fence of the potato patch a lady in crimson and ermine with a gold crown. They blinked, startled, and saw that there was no crimson and gold, only the dull clothes of the witch against the background of potato patch.

“And how did you get here?” Edred asked.

“That speckled hen of mine’s a-settin’ on the clock-face now,” she said. “I quieted her with a chalk-line drawn from her beak’s end straight out into the world of wonders. If she rouses up, then I’m back there, and I can’t never come back here, my dears, nor more than once, I can’t. So let’s make haste down to the Castle, and I’ll show you where my great granny see them put the treasure when she was a little gell.”

The three hurried down the steep-banked lane.

“Many’s the time,” the witch went on, “my granny pointed it out to me. It’s just alongside where﻿—”

And then the witch was not there any more. Edred and Elfrida were alone in the lane. The speckled hen must have recovered from her “quieting,” and got off the clock.

===Chinese===
接着，一刹那间，两个孩子全明白了，她也知道他们明白了。

「咦，」埃德雷德说，「您是那——」

「是呀。」她说，「我就是那个从远古来的巫婆。你们能回到过去，我就能走到前头来，因为只要我会造钟，那时和现在就是一回事。」

「您会造钟吗？」埃尔弗丽达说。「我还以为只有——」

「就是这么着。」巫婆说。「我不会造，可我认得会造的。我到这儿来就是找你们的，因为你们给我送过茶叶和糖。我有一双慧眼，我有。我能前看，也能后看。我朝前看了看，瞧见了你们；我朝后看了看，瞧见了你们找的东西，我知道宝藏在哪里，还有——」

「可是，您这些衣裳是哪儿弄来的？」埃德雷德问——这个问题，他日后可是要后悔的。

「哦，衣裳是顶容易到手的。」巫婆说。「要光说衣裳，我这会儿就能当头戴王冠的王后。」

孩子们恍惚瞥见，马铃薯地的十字篱笆前立着一位绯红貂裘、头戴金冠的贵妇。他们吃惊地眨了眨眼，看清了并没有什么绯红和金黄，只有巫婆那身暗沉沉的衣裳，衬着马铃薯地的背景。

「那您是怎么到这儿来的？」埃德雷德问。

「我那只花斑母鸡这会儿正蹲在钟面上呢。」她说。「我拿一条粉笔线，从她嘴尖一直画出去，直画进那个奇境里，才把她安顿住的。她要是惊起来，我就回到那边去了，就再也回不了这儿啦，我的乖乖，顶多再来一回，不能再多啦。所以咱们赶紧下城堡去吧，我领你们瞧瞧，我太奶奶小时候亲眼看见他们把宝藏藏在哪儿。」

三个人急忙顺着高坡夹峙的小路走下去。

「不知多少回，」巫婆接着说，「我奶奶都指给我看过。就在旁边，那儿——」

话没说完，巫婆就不在了。埃德雷德和埃尔弗丽达独自站在小路上。那只花斑母鸡准是从「安顿」里醒了过来，跳下钟去了。

===Original===
“She’s gone right enough,” said Edred, “and now we’ll never know. And just when she was going to tell us where it was. I do think it’s too jolly stupid for anything.”

“It’s *you* that’s too jolly stupid for anything,” said Elfrida hotly. “What did you want to go asking her about her silly clothes for? It was *that* did it. She’d have told us where it was before now if you hadn’t taken her time up with clothes. As if *clothes* mattered! I do wish to goodness you’d *sometimes* try to behave as if you’d got some sense.”

“Go it!” said Edred bitterly. “As if everything wasn’t tiresome enough. Now there’s another three days to wait, because of your nagging. Oh, it’s just exactly like a girl, so it is!”

“I’m﻿—I’m sorry,” said Elfrida, awestricken. “Let’s do something good to make up. I’ll give you that notebook of mine with the lead-pointed mother-of-pearl pencil, and we’ll go round to all the cottages and find out which are leaky, so as to be ready to patch them up when we’ve got the treasure.”

“I don’t *want* to be good,” said Edred bitterly. “*I* haven’t quarrelled and put everything back, but I’m going to now,” he said, with determination. “I don’t see why everything should be smashed up and me not said any of the things I want to say.”

“Oh, *don’t*!” cried Elfrida; “it’s bad enough to quarrel when you don’t want to, but to *set out* to quarrel! Don’t!”

Edred didn’t. He kicked the dust up with his boots, and the two went back to the Castle in gloomy silence.

At the gate Edred paused. “I’ll make it up now if you like,” he said. “I’ve only just thought of it﻿—but perhaps it’s three days from the *end* of the quarrel.”

“I see,” said Elfrida; “so the longer we keep it up﻿—”

“Yes,” said Edred; “so let’s call it Pax and not waste any *more* time.”

===Chinese===
「她是真走掉啦。」埃德雷德说，「这下咱们永远别想知道了。偏偏在她正要说地方的时候。我真是觉得，这蠢得没边儿了。」

「蠢得没边儿的是*你*。」埃尔弗丽达气冲冲地说。「你干吗偏要去问她那些蠢衣裳的事？坏就坏在*那上头*。要不是你拿衣裳的事占了她工夫，这会儿她早把地方告诉我们了。好像*衣裳*顶要紧似的！老天爷，我真希望你*偶尔*也试着装出一点脑子来。」

「骂呀，接着骂呀！」埃德雷德苦涩地说。「好像事情还不够烦人似的。这下又得再等三天，就因为你这唠叨。哦，这十足就是女孩子干的事，十足就是！」

「我——我对不起你。」埃尔弗丽达说，心里发怵。「咱们做件好事补过吧。我把那个本子连那支铅芯珠光笔送给你，咱们把所有的小屋都走一遍，查清楚哪几座漏雨，等得了宝藏，好准备把它们修补起来。」

「我*不要*当好人。」埃德雷德苦涩地说。「*我*没吵架，没把一切搅回去，可我现在就要吵了。」他横下心说。「我不明白，凭什么事事都砸了锅，倒不许我说一句我想说的话。」

「哦，*别*！」埃尔弗丽达喊道，「不想吵架还得吵，已经够糟了，还要*存心*去吵！别吵！」

埃德雷德没有吵。他用靴子踢起尘土，两人阴沉着脸，一声不响地回城堡去了。

到了大门口，埃德雷德站住了。「你要愿意，我现在就跟她和好。」他说。「我也是刚想到——说不定三天是从吵架*收场*算起的呢。」

「我明白了。」埃尔弗丽达说，「那么咱们越拖着不收场——」

「对。」埃德雷德说，「所以咱们就喊一声『休战』（Pax），别再浪费*更多的*时间啦。」

## IV The Landing of the French / 法国人的登陆

===Original===
Then they had dinner. The children had to sit very straight and eat very slowly, and their glasses were filled with beer instead of water; and when they asked for water Lady Arden asked how many more times they would have to be told that water was unwholesome. Lord Arden was very quiet. At quite the beginning of dinner he had told his wife all about the wise woman, and the landing of the French, and the three signs, and she had said, “Law, save us, my lord; you don’t say so?” and gone on placidly cutting up her meat. But when the cloth had been drawn, and decanters of wine placed among the dishes of dried plums and preserved pears, Lord Arden brought down his fist on the table and said—

“Not more than three glasses for me today, my lady. I am not superstitious, as well you know; but facts are facts. What did you do with that white Mouldiwarp?”

Elfrida had put it in the bottom drawer of the tallboys in her room (cook had told her which room that was), and said so rather timidly.

“It’s my belief,” said Lady Arden, who seemed to see what was her husband’s belief and to make it her own-a very winning quality—“it’s my belief that it’s a direct warning; in return, perhaps, for the tea and sugar.”

“Ah!” said Lord Arden. “Well, whether or no, every man in this village shall be armed and paraded this day, or I’ll know the reason why. I’m not going to have the French stepping ashore as cool as cucumbers, without ‘With your leave,’ or ‘By your leave,’ and anyone to say afterwards, ‘Well, Arden, you had fair warning, only you would know best.’”

===Chinese===
然后他们吃晚饭。两个孩子得坐得笔直、吃得极慢，杯子里斟的是啤酒而不是水；他们讨水喝的时候，阿登夫人就问，还得再告诉他们多少回，水是于身体有害的东西。阿登勋爵一言不发。席面刚开的时候，他就把巫婆的事、法国人登陆的事、三个征兆的事，原原本本告诉了夫人；她说：「哎呀老天爷，我的老爷；您不是说着玩的吧？」说罢照旧安安静静地切她的肉。可是等到桌布撤去，几瓶葡萄酒摆在干李子和糖渍梨的碟子中间，阿登勋爵一拳捶在桌子上，说道——

「今天我顶多喝三杯，夫人。我不是迷信的人，这您是知道的；可事实总归是事实。你把那只白鼹穆迪沃普怎么处置了？」

埃尔弗丽达把它放进了她屋里那口高脚柜最底层的抽屉（哪间屋子是她住的，还是厨娘告诉她的），她怯生生地这么回了一句。

「依我看哪，」阿登夫人说——她仿佛总能看穿丈夫信什么，便也把那当成自己信的，这可是种顶讨人喜欢的品性——「依我看，这是一桩直接的警示；没准儿，是回报那茶叶和糖的。」

「啊！」阿登勋爵说。「哼，不管是不是，今儿这一天，村里每一个男人都得拿起武器、列队操演，不然我倒要问出个缘故来。我可不打算让法国人像个没事人似的登了岸，不道一声『可否请您恩准』，不说一句『借光、借光』，回头还有人来说：『嗐，阿登，人家明明白白警示过你，只是你偏要自作主张。』」

===Original===
“No,” said Lady Arden, “that *would* be unpleasant.”

Lord Arden’s decision was made stronger by the arrival of a man on a very hot horse.

“The French are coming,” he said, quite out of breath. But he could not say how he knew. “They all say so,” was all that could be got out of him, and “They told me to come tell you, my lord, and what’s us to do?”

===Chinese===
「是啊，」阿登夫人说，「那*可就*不痛快了。」

一个人骑着匹热气腾腾的马赶到，阿登勋爵的决心愈发坚定了。

「法国人来啦。」他说，话都喘得囫囵不了。可他说不上来自己是怎么知道的。「大伙儿都这么说。」——从他嘴里只掏得出这一句，外加一句：「他们叫我来禀报老爷，咱们大伙儿可怎么办哪？」

===Original===
We live so safely now; we have nothing to be afraid of. When we have wars they are not in our own country. The police look after burglars, and even thunder is attended to by lightning-rods. It is not easy for us to understand the frantic terror of those times, when, from day to day, every man, woman, and child trembled in its shoes for fear lest “the French should come”—the French, led by Boney. Boney, to us, is Napoleon Bonaparte, a little person in a cocked hat out of the history books. To those who lived in England when he was a man alive, he was “the Terror that walked by night,” making children afraid to go to bed, and causing strong men to sleep in their boots, with sword and pistol by the bed-head, within easy reach of the newly awakened hand.

===Chinese===
如今我们活得多么安稳；我们没有什么可怕的。纵然有战争，也轮不到在我们自己的国土上打。盗贼有警察照管，连雷公也有人拿避雷针招呼。我们很难体会那个年代的疯狂恐怖——那时候，日复一日，每一个男人、女人和孩子都吓得两腿发颤，唯恐「法国人打过来」——由波尼统率的法国人。在我们眼里，波尼就是拿破仑·波拿巴，历史书里一个戴双角帽的小个子。可在他还活在世上、英国人还与他同时代的日子，他是「黑夜行走的恐怖」，吓得孩子不敢上床，弄得壮汉和衣而睡、靴子都不脱，剑和手枪搁在床头，好教刚惊醒的手一伸就够得着。

===Original===
Edred and Elfrida began to understand a little, when they saw how the foretelling of the wise woman, strengthened by the rumours that began to run about like rats in every house in the village, stirred the people to the wildest activity.

Lord Arden was so busy giving orders, and my lady so busy talking his orders over with the maidservants, that the children were left free to use their eyes and ears. And they went down into the village and saw many strange things. They saw men at the grindstone sharpening old swords, and others who had no swords putting a fine edge on billhooks, hatchets, scythes, and kitchen choppers. They saw other men boarding up their windows and digging holes in their gardens and burying their money and their teaspoons in the holes. No one knew how the rumour had begun, but everyone believed it now.

They went in and out of the cottages as they chose. Everyone seemed to know them and to be pleased, in an absent sort of way, to see them, but nobody had time to talk to them, so they soon lost the fear they had had at first of being found out to be not the people they were being taken for. They found the women busy brushing and mending old scarlet coats and tight white trousers, and all along the dip of the cliff men were posted, with spyglasses, looking out to sea. Other men toiled up the slope with great bundles of brown brushwood and dried furze on their backs, and those bundles were piled high, ready to be lighted the moment it should be certain that the French were coming.

===Chinese===
埃德雷德和埃尔弗丽达这才开始有点明白了：巫婆的预言，加上像耗子似的在村里家家户户乱窜的流言一发酵，把人们搅得热火朝天、忙乱到了极点。

阿登勋爵忙于发号施令，夫人忙于同女仆们商议这些号令，两个孩子便得了自由，去使唤自己的眼睛和耳朵。他们下到村里，见到了许多稀奇事。他们看见男人们在砂轮上磨旧剑，没有剑的，把砍刀、斧头、长镰和厨房剁骨刀磨得飞快。他们看见另一些人钉死窗户，在园子里挖坑，把钱财和茶匙埋进坑里。谁也不知道流言是怎么起的头，可如今人人都信了。

他们在各处村舍里进进出出，随意得很。人人都像是认得他们，见了他们也高兴——一种心不在焉的高兴——可谁也没有工夫搭理他们，于是他们起初那种生怕被识破、被人发现并不是「被当成的那两个人」的恐惧，很快就丢掉了。他们看见妇女们忙着刷洗缝补旧的猩红军衣和绷紧的白裤子；沿着悬崖的凹处，一处处布置着人，举着望远镜瞭望大海。另一些男人背着大捆大捆的褐色柴枝和干荆豆柴，吭哧吭哧爬上坡，柴捆堆得老高，只等确证法国人一来，便立刻点火。

===Original===
Elfrida wished more than ever that she knew more about the later chapters of the history book. Did Boney land in England on the 17th of June, 1807? She could not remember. There was something, she knew, in the book about a French invasion, but she could not remember what it was an invasion of, nor when it took place. So she and Edred knew as little as anyone else what really *was* going to happen. The Mouldiwarp, in the hurried interview she had had with it before dinner, had promised to come if she called it, “With poetry, of course,” it added, as it curled up in the corner of the drawer, and this comforted her a good deal when, going up to get her bonnet, she found the bottom drawer empty. So, though she was as interested as Edred in all that was going on, it was only with half her mind. The other half was busy trying to make up a piece of poetry, so that any emergency which might suddenly arise would not find her powerless because poetry-less.

So for once Edred was more observant than she, and when he noticed that the men built a bonfire not at all on the spot which Lord Arden had pointed out as most convenient, he wondered why.

===Chinese===
埃尔弗丽达比什么时候都更巴望自己多知道些历史课本后半部的章节。波尼可曾在 1807 年 6 月 17 日在英国登陆？她记不得了。她知道书里讲到过一场法国人的入侵，可她记不得入侵的是什么地方，也记不得发生在哪年哪月。于是，眼下究竟*要*出什么事，她和埃德雷德跟旁人一样两眼一抹黑。晚饭前她匆匆见穆迪沃普的那一面里，它答应过她一叫就来，「当然，得用诗。」它蜷在抽屉角落里补了这么一句。后来她上楼取软帽，发现底层抽屉已空空如也，这句话给了她莫大的安慰。所以，尽管眼下的种种热闹她跟埃德雷德一样关切，却也只用得上一半心思。另一半正忙着作诗，好教任何可能突然临头的急难，都不至于因为她腹中无诗而拿她没办法。

于是破天荒头一遭，埃德雷德比她观察得细。他注意到人们堆篝火，压根儿不堆在阿登勋爵指出的那个顶方便的地点，心里纳闷这是为什么。

===Original===
And presently, seeing a man going by that very spot, he asked him why. To his surprise, the man at once poked him in the ribs with a very hard finger, and said—

“Ah, you’re a little wag, you are! But you’re a little gentleman, too, and so’s the little lady, bless her. You never gave us away to the Preventives—for all you found out.”

“Of course,” said Elfrida cautiously, “we should never give anyone away.”

“Want to come along down now?” the man asked. He was a brown-faced, sturdy, sailor-looking man, with a short pigtail sticking out from the back of his head like the china handle of a Japanese teapot.

“Oh, yes,” said Elfrida, and Edred did not say “Oh, no.”

“Then just you wait till I’m out of sight, and then come down the way you see me go. Go long same as if you was after butterflies or the like—a bit this way and a bit that—see?” said the man. And they obeyed.

===Chinese===
不多时，见一个人正打那地点旁边经过，他便上去问缘故。叫他吃一惊的是，那人立刻用一根硬邦邦的手指头戳了戳他的肋骨，说道——

「嗬，你真是个小促狭鬼，没错！不过你也是位小绅士，小姑娘呢，也是位小贵小姐，上帝保佑她。你们虽说把底细摸了个透，可从来没向缉私队（the Preventives）告过密。」

「那自然，」埃尔弗丽达字斟句酌地说，「我们从不告发任何人。」

「这会儿想不想跟我们一块儿下去？」那人问。他是个棕红脸膛、结结实实、水手模样的人，脑后翘着一根短辫子，活像日本茶壶上的瓷提梁。

「哦，想。」埃尔弗丽达说；埃德雷德也没有说「哦，不想」。

「那你们就先等我走没了影儿，再照你们瞧我走的样子下来。一路逛逛悠悠的，就当是追蝴蝶什么的——这么拐一下，那么拐一下——明白？」那人说。两人便照办了。

===Original===
Alas! too few children in those uninteresting times of ours have ever been in a smuggler’s cave. To Edred and to Elfrida it was as great a novelty as it would be to you or to me.

When they came up with the brown man he was crouching in the middle of a patch of furze.

“Jump they outside bushes,” he said. And they jumped, and wound their way among the furze-bushes by little narrow rabbit-paths till they stood by his side.

Then he lifted a great heap of furze and bramble that looked as if it had lived and died exactly where it was. And there was a hole—with steps going down.

It was dark below, but Elfrida did not hesitate to do as she was told and to go forward. And if Edred hesitated it was only for a minute.

The children went down some half a dozen steps. Then the brown man came into the hole too, and drew the furze after him. And he lighted a lantern; there was a tallow candle in it, and it smelt very nasty indeed. But what are smells, even those of hot tallow and hot iron, compared with the splendid exploring of a smuggler’s cave? It was everything the children had ever dreamed of—and more.

===Chinese===
呜呼！在我们这乏味无聊的年代，进过走私犯洞穴的孩子实在太少了。对埃德雷德和埃尔弗丽达，这桩事的新鲜劲儿，就跟搁在你我身上一样大。

他们赶上那棕红脸膛的人时，他正蹲在一片荆豆丛当中。

「跳进外圈这些丛子里来。」他说。他们便跳了进去，顺着一条条窄小的兔子小径，在荆豆丛间绕来绕去，绕到他身边站定。

随后他掀起一大堆荆豆和悬钩子，那堆东西看上去就像生在原地、死在原地的。底下是一个洞口——有台阶往下走。

下面黑黢黢的，可埃尔弗丽达毫不迟疑，照着吩咐往前走。埃德雷德若是踌躇了，也只踌躇了一分钟。

两个孩子下了五六级台阶。随后那棕红脸膛的人也钻进洞来，把荆豆拖回身后掩好。他点起一盏提灯；灯里是支牛油蜡烛，那气味实在够呛。可是气味算什么？哪怕是热牛油和热铁皮的气味，比起勘探走私犯洞穴这桩光灿灿的大事业来，又算得了什么？这洞里的一切，是孩子们做梦梦到过的全部——还要多。

===Original===
There was the slow descent with the yellowness of the lantern flame casting golden lights and inky shadows on the smooth whiteness of the passage’s chalk walls. There were steps, there was a rude heavy door, fastened by a great lock and a key to open it—as big as a church key. And when the door had creaked open there was the great cave. It was so high that you could not see the roof—only darkness. Out of an opening in the chalk at the upper end a stream of water fell, slid along a smooth channel down the middle of the cave and ran along down a steep incline, rather like a small railway cutting, and disappeared under a low arch.

“So there’d always be water if you had to stand a siege,” said Edred.

On both sides of the great cave barrels and bales were heaped on a sanded floor. There were a table and benches cut out of solid chalk, and an irregular opening partly blocked by a mass of fallen cliff, through which you saw the mysterious twilit sea, with stars coming out over it.

You saw this, and you felt—quite suddenly, too—a wild wind that pressed itself against you like a wrestler trying a fall, and whistled in your ears and drove you back to the big cave, out of breath and panting.

===Chinese===
一路缓缓下行，提灯火焰那昏黄的光，在过道白垩墙壁光滑的白面上投下金色的亮块和墨黑的影子。有台阶，有一扇粗笨沉重的大门，一把大锁锁着，开锁的钥匙——有教堂钥匙那么大。门吱呀一声推开，大洞就在眼前了。洞顶高得望不见——只有黑沉沉一片。一股水从顶头白垩岩的一个开口落下来，顺着洞中央一道光滑的水槽流淌，又沿一条陡坡泻下去，颇像一道小小的铁路路堑，末了钻进一座矮拱门底下不见了。

「这么说，万一要固守待援，水是永远不缺的。」埃德雷德说。

大洞两侧，酒桶和货捆堆在铺着细沙的地面上。有一张桌子和几条长凳，是从整块白垩岩里凿出来的；还有一个不规则的豁口，半叫一坨塌下来的崖石堵着，从那儿望出去，能看见暮色苍茫、神秘莫测的大海，海面上正缀起点点星光。

你看见这片景致，还感觉到——也是猝不及防地——一阵狂野的风扑上身来，像个摔跤手使绊子似的紧紧抵住你，在你耳边呼啸，把你逼回大洞里去，气喘吁吁，上气不接下气。

===Original===
“There’ll be half a gale tonight,” said the smuggler; for such, no doubt, he was.

“Do you think the French *will* land tomorrow in Lymchurch Bay?” Edred asked.

By the light of the lantern the smuggler solemnly winked.

“You two can keep a secret, I know,” he said. “The French won’t land; it’s us what’ll land, and we’ll land here and not in bay; and what we’ll land is a good drop of the real thing, and a yard or two of silk or lace maybe. I don’t know who ’twas put it about as the French was a-coming, but you may lay to it they aren’t no friends of the Revenue.”

“Oh, I see,” said Elfrida. “And did—”

“The worst of it’ll be the lookout they’ll keep. Lucky for us it’s all our men as has volunteered for duty. And we know our friends.”

===Chinese===
「今儿夜里有半场大风。」那走私犯说；他无疑正是干这一行的。

「你看法国人明天*当真*会在利姆彻奇湾登陆吗？」埃德雷德问。

就着提灯的光，那走私犯煞有介事地眨了眨眼。

「你们俩嘴严，这个我知道。」他说。「法国人不会来；要登陆的是我们，而且在这儿上岸，不上湾里去；我们运上岸的，是足足一口正宗的好货，兴许还有一两码绸子或者花边。是谁放出话来说法国人要来，我不知道；可你把这话搁在心里——那起子人决不会是缉私官（the Revenue）的朋友。」

「哦，我明白了。」埃尔弗丽达说。「那么可曾——」

「麻烦就麻烦在他们要布的岗哨。亏得报了名当差的，全是我们自己人。自己人我们心里都有数。」

===Original===
“But do you mean,” said Edred, “that you can be friends with a Frenchman, when we’re at war with them?”

“It’s like this, little man,” said the smuggler, sitting down on a keg that stood handily on its head ready for a seat. “We ain’t no quarrel with the free-trade men—neither here nor there. A man’s got his living to get, hasn’t he now? So you see a man’s trade comes first—what he gets his bread by. So you see these chaps as meet us mid-channel and hand us the stuff—they’re free traders first and Frenchies after—the same like we’re merchants before all. We ain’t no quarrel with them. It’s the French soldiers we’re at war with, not the honest French traders that’s in the same boat as us ourselves.”

“Then somebody’s just made up about Boney coming, so as to keep people busy in the bay while you’re smuggling here?” said Edred.

“I wouldn’t go so far as that, sir,” said the man, “but if it did happen that way it ’ud be a sort of special dispensation for us free-trade men that get our living by honest work and honest danger; that’s all I say, knowing by what’s gone before that you two are safe as any old salt afloat.”

The two children would have given a good deal to know what it was that had “gone before.” But they never did know. And sometimes, even now, they wonder what it was that the Edred and Elfrida of those days had done to win the confidence of this swaggering smuggler. They both think, and I daresay they are right, that it must have been something rather fine.

===Chinese===
「可你的意思是，」埃德雷德说，「咱们正跟法国人打仗，你却能跟法国人做朋友？」

「是这么回事，小先生。」走私犯说着，在一小桶上坐下来——那桶恰巧倒立着，天生就是现成的座位。「我们跟做自由买卖的没有冤仇——哪儿都没有。人总得谋生计，是不是这个理儿？所以你瞧，人的行当是头一位的——那是他挣面包的门路。所以你瞧，海峡当中跟我们碰头、把货交到我们手上的那些个伙计——他们先是自由买卖人，然后才是法国佬——就跟我们头一位是买卖人一个样。我们跟他们没冤仇。咱们打仗打的是法国兵，不是那些跟咱们同乘一条船的老老实实的法国买卖人。」

「那么说，是有人编排出波尼要来的谣言，好让人们在湾里忙作一团，你们就在这儿走私？」埃德雷德说。

「话我不敢说到那份上，少爷。」那人说，「不过事情真要是那么凑巧，对我们这些靠老实的营生、老实的风险挣饭吃的自由买卖人来说，倒算得一种特别的眷顾；我就说这一句——凭先前那些事，我知道你们俩嘴严得很，跟船上任何老水手一样靠得住。」

那「先前的事」到底是什么，两个孩子情愿拿许多东西来换这个答案。可他们始终没能知道。直到如今，他们有时还纳闷：当年的那对埃德雷德和埃尔弗丽达，究竟做了什么，才赢得了这位神气活现的走私犯的信任。他们俩都认定——我敢说他们想得不错——那必定是件顶漂亮的事。

===Original===
Having seen all the ins and outs of the cave, the children were not sorry to get back to Arden Castle, for it was now dark, and long past their proper bedtime, and it really had been rather a wearing day.

They were put to bed, rather severely, by Lady Arden’s own maid, whom they had not met before and did not want to meet again—so shrivelled and dry and harsh was she. And they slept like happy little tops, in the coarse homespun linen sheets scented with lavender grown in the castle garden, that were spread over soft, fat, pincushion-beds, filled with the feathers of geese eaten at the castle table.

Only Elfrida woke once and found the room filled with red light, and, looking out of the window, saw that one of the beacon bonfires was alight and that the flames and smoke were streaming across the dark summer sky-driven by the wind that shouted and yelled and shook the windows, and was by this time, she felt sure, at least three-quarters of a gale. The beacon was lighted; therefore the French were coming. And Elfrida yawned and went back to bed. She was too sleepy to believe in Boney. But at that time, a hundred years ago, hundreds of little children shivered and cried in their beds, being quite sure that now at last all the dreadful prophecies of mothers and nurses would come true, and that Boney, in all his mysterious, unknown horror, would really now, at last, “have them.”

===Chinese===
把洞里的犄角旮旯看了个遍，两个孩子也就不嫌回阿登城堡的路远了；这时天已黑透，早过了他们正经的睡觉钟点，这一天实在也够累人的。

伺候他们上床的是阿登夫人的贴身女仆，态度相当严厉——这女仆他们先前没见过，也不打算再见到第二回——她是那样干瘪、枯燥、冷硬。他们睡得像两只快活的小陀螺，床上铺的是粗纺亚麻床单，带着城堡花园里薰衣草的香气，罩在软乎乎、胖鼓鼓、针插似的床垫上——床垫里填的，是城堡饭桌上吃过的那些鹅的羽毛。

只有埃尔弗丽达醒了一回，发觉满屋红光。她朝窗外一望，看见一座烽火篝火已经点燃，火焰和浓烟横扫过黑沉沉的夏夜天空——那风在吼、在叫、在摇撼窗户，此时此刻，她敢肯定，至少已有四分之三场大风。烽火点燃了；如此说来，法国人要来了。埃尔弗丽达打了个哈欠，回床上接着睡。她困得顾不上信波尼了。可在一百年前的那个时候，成百上千的小孩子在被窝里发抖、哭喊，满心笃定：母亲和奶妈那些可怕的预言，这回终于要应验了；波尼带着他那一身神秘莫测、无人识得的恐怖，这回可真的、终于要「来抓他们」了。

===Original===
It was grey morning when the wind, wearied of the silly resistance of the leaded window, suddenly put forth his strength, tore the window from its hinges, drove it across the window frame, and swept through the room, flapping the bedclothes like wet sails, and wakening the children most thoroughly, far beyond any hope of “one more snooze.” They got up and dressed. No one was about in the house, but the front door was open. It was quite calm on that side, but as soon as the children left the shelter of the castle wall the wind caught at them, hit, slapped, drove, worried, beat them, till they had hard work to stand upright, and getting along was very slow and difficult. Yet they made their way somehow to the cliff, where a thick, black crowd stood—a crowd that was not really black when you got quite close and could look at it in the grey dawn—light, but rather brilliantly red, white, and blue, like the Union Jack, because they were the armed men in their makeshift uniforms whom old Lord Arden had drilled and paraded the evening before. And they were all looking out to sea.

===Chinese===
到了灰蒙蒙的早晨，那风终于腻烦了铅框窗户傻乎乎的抵抗，猛然使出力气，把窗户从铰链上扯下来，整个儿推进窗框那头，随即扫进屋子，把床单被褥拍打得像湿漉漉的船帆，把两个孩子彻底唤醒——唤醒得干干净净，半点「再眯一会儿」的指望也不剩。他们起床穿衣。屋里一个人影也没有，可大门敞着。大门这一面倒是风平浪静，但孩子们一离开城堡墙的庇护，风就揪住他们，撞、掴、推、拧、捶，直捶得他们连站稳都要费尽气力，往前挪一步都又慢又难。可他们好歹还是挪到了悬崖边。那儿黑压压站着一厚层人群——等你走到近前，在灰色晨光里细看，那人群其实并不黑——颜色浅淡，而且红、白、蓝得相当鲜亮，像一面米字旗，因为那是前一夜老阿登勋爵操练检阅过的武装村民，穿着他们临时的制服。他们都朝大海了望着。

===Original===
The sea was like the inside of an oyster-shell, barred with ridges of cold silver, the sky above was grey as a gull’s wing, and between sea and sky a ship was driving straight on to the rocks a hundred feet below.

“’Tis a French ship, by her rig,” someone said.

“The first of the fleet—a scout,” said another, “and Heaven has sent a storm to destroy them like it destroyed the accursed Armada in Queen Bess’s time.”

===Chinese===
大海像牡蛎壳的里子，一道道冷银色的棱纹横亘着；头顶的天灰得像鸥鸟的翅膀；而海天之间，一条船正笔直地朝下面一百英尺的礁石冲去。

「瞧那帆装，是条法国船。」有人说。

「舰队的头一条——条斥候船。」另一人说，「上天降下一场风暴来消灭他们，就像贝丝女王（Queen Bess）年间消灭那支该死的无敌舰队一样。」

===Original===
And still the ship came nearer.

“’Tis the *Bonne Esperance*,” said the low voice of the smuggler friend close to Elfrida’s ear, and she could only just hear him through the whistling of the gale. “’Tis true what old Betty said; the French will land here today—but they’ll land dead corpses. And all our little cargo—they’ve missed our boat in the gale—it’ll all be smashed to bits afore our eyes. It’s poor work being a honest merchant.”

The men in their queer uniforms, carrying their queer weapons, huddled closer together, and all eyes were fixed on the ship as it came on and on.

“Is it *sure* to be wrecked?” whispered Elfrida, catching at old Lord Arden’s hand.

“No hope, my child. Get you home to bed,” he said.

It did not make any difference that all this had happened a hundred years ago. There was the cold, furious sea lashing the rocks far down below the cliff. Elfrida could not bear to stay and see that ship smash on the rocks as her carved work-box had smashed when she dropped it on the kitchen bricks. She could not even bear to think of seeing it. Poetry was difficult, but to stay here and see a ship wrecked—a ship that had men aboard—was more difficult still.

===Chinese===
那条船还是越来越近。

「那是《美好希望号》（*Bonne Esperance*）。」走私犯朋友的声音贴着埃尔弗丽达的耳朵低低响起，风声呼啸里她勉强才听得见。「老贝蒂说的是真的；法国人今天要在这儿登陆——可登陆的是一具具尸首。还有咱们那一小船货——大风里他们没瞧见咱们的船——全要在咱们眼面前摔个粉碎。做个老老实实的买卖人，真是可怜的营生。」

穿着古怪制服、扛着古怪武器的汉子们挤得更紧了，所有的眼睛都钉在那条一个劲儿驶近的船上。

「它*一定*会撞碎吗？」埃尔弗丽达悄声问，一把抓住老阿登勋爵的手。

「没指望了，我的孩子。你回家睡觉去吧。」他说。

这一切纵然发生在一百年前，也一点儿不能叫人好受些。底下深处，冰冷狂怒的大海正鞭打着礁石。埃尔弗丽达受不了待在这儿，眼看那条船在礁石上撞碎——就像她那只雕花针线盒掉在厨房砖地上摔碎的那样。她连想一想都受不了。作诗是难的；可待在这儿看一条船失事——一条船上有人的船——更是难上加难。

===Original===
> “Oh, Mouldiwarp, do come to me;

 I cannot bear it, do you see,”

was not, perhaps, fine poetry, but it expressed her feelings exactly, and, anyhow, it did what it was meant to do. The white mole rubbed against her ankles even as she spoke. She caught it up.

===Chinese===
> 「哦，穆迪沃普，快来我这里；
> 我受不了了，你可知道，」

这或许算不上好诗，可它把她的心思说得再准不过，而且不管怎样，它办成了它该办的事。她话音未落，白鼹已经蹭上了她的脚踝。她一把将它抱起。

===Original===
“Oh, what are we to do?”

“Go home,” it said, “to the castle—you’ll find the door now.”

And they turned to go. And as they turned they heard a grinding crunch, mixed with the noise of the waves and winds, enormously louder, but yet just the sort of noise a dog makes when he is eating the bones of the chicken you had for dinner and gets the chicken’s ribs all at once into his mouth. Then there was a sort of sighing moan from the crowd on the cliff, who had been there all night for the French to land, and then Lord Arden’s voice—

“The French have landed. She spoke the truth. The French have landed—Heaven help them!”

And as the children ran towards the house they knew that every man in that crowd would now be ready to risk his life to save from the sea those Frenchies whom they had sat up all night to kill with swords and scythes and bills and meat-choppers. Men are queer creatures!

===Chinese===
「哦，咱们该怎么办呢？」

「回家去。」它说，「回城堡去——这会儿你们找得着门了。」

他们转身要走。刚一转身，就听见一声轧轧的巨响，混在风浪的喧嚣里，响得无以复加——却又活像一条狗嚼你们晚饭吃剩的鸡骨头、一口把整副鸡肋咬进嘴里的那种声响。跟着，悬崖上守了一整夜等法国人登陆的人群发出一阵叹息似的呜咽，随后是阿登勋爵的声音——

「法国人登陆了。她说的是真话。法国人登陆了——上天保佑他们吧！」

孩子们朝宅子跑去，一路心里明白：那人群里的每一条汉子，此刻都情愿舍出性命，把那些法国佬从海里救起来——就在昨夜，他们还熬夜守着，准备用刀剑、长镰、钩刀和剁肉刀杀了他们呢。人真是些奇怪的玩意儿！

===Original===
To get out of it—back to the safe quiet of a life without shipwrecks and witches—that was all Elfrida wanted. Holding the mole in one hand and dragging Edred by the other, she got back to the castle and in at the open front door, up the stairs, and straight to a door—she knew it would be the right one, and it was.

There was the large attic with the beams, and the long, wonderful row of chests under the sloping roof. And the moment the door was shut, the raging noise of the winds ceased, as the flaring noise of gas ceases when you turn it off. And now once more the golden light filtered through the chinks of the tiles, and outside was the “tick, tick” of moving pigeon feet, the rustling of pigeon feathers, and the cooroocoo of pigeon voices.

On the ground lay their own clothes. “Change,” said the white mole, a little out of breath because it had been held very tight and carried very fast.

And the moment they began to put on their own clothes it seemed that the pigeon noises came closer and closer, and somehow helped them out of the prickly clothes of 1807 and back into the comfortable sailor suits of 1907.

===Chinese===
脱身出去——回到没有海难也没有巫婆的、安稳清静的生活里去——埃尔弗丽达想要的仅此而已。她一手抱着鼹鼠，一手拽着埃德雷德，回到城堡，从敞开的大门进去，上楼，径直奔向一扇门——她知道准是这一扇，也果然是。

还是那间有大梁的大阁楼，斜屋顶下还是那长长一排妙不可言的箱子。门一关上，狂风的怒号便戛然而止，就像你拧熄煤气时，煤气的呼呼声应声而灭。金色的光再一次从瓦缝里筛下来，外面是鸽子脚走动的「嗒、嗒」声，鸽子羽毛的窸窣声，还有鸽子嗓门的咕噜嘟。

地上放着他们自己的衣裳。「换衣裳。」白鼹说，有点儿喘不过气——因为它一路被搂得太紧、带得太急了。

而他们一开始换上自己的衣裳，鸽子的声响便仿佛越来越近、越来越近，不知怎么就把他们从 1807 年扎人的衣裳里拱了出来，拱回了 1907 年舒舒服服的水手装里。

===Original===
“Did ye find the treasure?” the mole asked, and the children answered—

“Why no; we never thought of it.”

“It don’t make no odds,” said the mole. “’Twaren’t dere.”

“There?” said Elfrida. “Then we’re *here*? We’re *now* again, I mean? We’re not then?”

“Oh, you’re *now*, sure enough,” said the mole, “and won’t you catch it! Dame Honeysett’s been raising the countryside arter ye. Next time ye go gallivantin’ into old ancient days you’d best set the clock back. Young folks don’t know everything. Get along down and take your scolding.

===Chinese===
「寻着宝藏了没有？」鼹鼠问。孩子们答道——

「没呢；我们压根儿没想到它。」

「想没想到也不打紧。」鼹鼠说。「那儿压根儿就没有。」

「那儿？」埃尔弗丽达说。「那么咱们是回到*这儿*了？我是说，咱们又回到*现在*了？不是那时候了？」

「哦，你们是回到*现在*了，错不了。」鼹鼠说，「可有你们好受的！哈尼塞特婆婆（Dame Honeysett）为了找你们，把四乡八邻都惊动遍了。下回再溜到老古的年代去闲逛，顶好先把钟往回拨一拨。年轻人啊，到底不是什么都懂。快下去，挨你们那顿骂吧。

===Original===
> “What must be must.

 If you can’t get crumb, you must put up with crust.

Goodbye.”

It ran under one of the chests, and Edred and Elfrida were left looking at each other in the attic between the rows of chests.

“Do you *like* adventures?” said Edred slowly.

“Yes,” said Elfrida firmly; “and so do you. Come along down.”

===Chinese===
> 「该来的总归要来。
> 吃不上面包心，就将就着啃面包皮。
再会。」

它从一只箱子底下钻走了，埃德雷德和埃尔弗丽达被撇在阁楼上，在那一排排箱子中间面面相觑。

「你*喜欢*冒险吗？」埃德雷德慢吞吞地说。

「喜欢。」埃尔弗丽达斩钉截铁地说；「你也一样。走，下去吧。」

## II The Mouldiwarp / 白鼹穆迪沃普

===Original===
And it was—it was the living image of the little pig-like animal that was stamped in gold above the chequered shield on the cover of the white book in which they had found the spell. And as on the yellowy white of the vellum book-cover, so here on the thymy grass of the knoll it shone golden. The children stood perfectly still. They were afraid to move lest they should scare away this little creature which, though golden, was alive and moved about at their feet, turning a restless nose to right and left.

“It *is*,” said Elfrida again, very softly, so as not to frighten it.

“*What?*” Edred asked, though he knew well enough.

“Off the book that we got the spell out of.”

“That was our crest on the top of our coat-of-arms, like on the old snuffbox that was great-grandpapa’s.”

“Well, this is our crest come alive, that’s all.”

===Chinese===
正是它——正是那头小猪模样的动物的活生生的形象，他们找到咒语的那本白皮书，封面上方格纹盾牌的顶端就烫金印着这动物。正如它在羊皮封面的黄白色底子上闪着金光一样，此刻在阿登丘长满百里香的草皮上，它也金光闪闪。孩子们一动也不动地站着。他们不敢挪动，生怕吓跑这个小生灵——它虽然是金色的，却是活的，在他们脚边爬来爬去，一只不安分的鼻子左探右探。

「*就是*它。」埃尔弗丽达又说了一遍，声音放得极轻，生怕惊着它。

「*什么呀？*」埃德雷德问，其实他心里再清楚不过。

「就是咱们找咒语的那本书上印的那只。」

「那是咱们的家徽，在纹章的顶上，就跟曾祖父那只旧鼻烟壶上的一样。」

「瞧，这不就是咱们的家徽活了嘛，就这么回事。」

===Original===
“Don’t you be too clever,” said Edred. “It said *badge*; I don’t believe badge is the same thing as crest. A badge is leeks, or roses, or thistles—something you can wear in your cap. *I* shouldn’t like to wear *that* in my cap.”

And still the golden thing at their feet moved cautiously and without ceasing.

“Why,” said Edred suddenly, “it’s just a common old mole.”

“It isn’t; it’s our own crest, that’s on the spoons and things. It’s our own old family mole that’s our crest. How can it be a common mole? It’s all golden.”

And, even as she spoke, it left off being golden. For the last bit of sun dipped behind the shoulder of the downs, and in the grey twilight that was left the mole was white—anyone could see that.

“Oh!” said Elfrida—but she stuck to her point. “So you see,” she went on, “it can’t be just a really-mole. Really-moles are black.”

===Chinese===
「你呀，可别太自作聪明。」埃德雷德说。「咒语里写的是*徽记*；我不信徽记跟家徽是一回事。徽记是韭葱啊、玫瑰啊、蓟啊之类的东西——是能别在帽子上戴的。*我*可不想把*这个*别在帽子上。」

他们脚边那个金色的东西仍旧小心翼翼地动着，一刻不停。

「哎呀，」埃德雷德忽然说，「这不就是一只普普通通的老鼹鼠嘛。」

「才不是呢；它是咱们自家的家徽，就印在那些勺子什么的上头。是咱们自家那只当了家徽的老鼹鼠。它怎么会是普通鼹鼠？它浑身金黄金黄的。」

话音未落，它就不再是金色的了。因为最后一线太阳沉到了丘原的坡脊后面，剩下的灰色暮光里，那只鼹鼠是白色的——谁都看得出来。

「噢！」埃尔弗丽达叫了一声——可她仍不肯让步。「所以你瞧，」她接着说，「它不可能是一只真正的鼹鼠。真正的鼹鼠是黑的。」

===Original===
“Well,” said Edred, “it’s very tame. I will say that.”

“Well—” Edred was beginning; but at that same moment the mole also, suddenly and astonishingly, said, “Well?”

There was a hushed pause. Then—

“Did *you* say that?” Elfrida whispered.

“No,” said Edred, “*you* did.”

“Don’t whisper, now,” said the mole; “ ’tain’t purty manners, so I tells ’ee.”

===Chinese===
「好吧，」埃德雷德说，「它倒是很温顺。这一点我认。」

「那么——」埃德雷德刚要往下说；可就在同一瞬间，那只鼹鼠也突然地、令人惊讶地开了口：「那么？」

一阵屏息的停顿。接着——

「是*你*说的吗？」埃尔弗丽达悄声问。

「不是我，」埃德雷德说，「是*你*说的。」

「别交头接耳的。」鼹鼠说，「那样子可不体面，俺跟你们说。」

===Original===
With one accord the two children came to their knees, one on each side of the white mole.

“I *say*!” said Edred.

“Now, don’t,” said the mole, pointing its nose at him quite as disdainfully as any human being could have pointed a finger. “Don’t you go for to pretend you don’t know as Mouldiwarps ’as got tongues in dere heads same’s what you’ve got.”

“But not to talk with?” said Elfrida softly.

“Don’t you tell me,” said the Mouldiwarp, bristling a little. “Hasn’t no one told you e’er a fairy tale? All us beastes has tongues, and when we’re dere us uses of en.”

“When you’re where?” said Edred, rather annoyed at being forced to believe in fairy tales, which he had never really liked.

===Chinese===
两个孩子不约而同地跪了下来，在那只白鼹鼠两侧一边一个。

「我*说*！」埃德雷德说。

「得了，别装了。」鼹鼠说，把鼻子朝他一指，那份轻蔑劲儿，不输任何人竖起一根手指指着人。「你们可别装模作样，装作不知道穆迪沃普（Mouldiwarp，「鼹鼠」的古称）的脑袋里也长着舌头，跟你们脑袋里长的一样。」

「可那不是用来说话的呀？」埃尔弗丽达轻声说。

「少来这套。」白鼹穆迪沃普说，身上的毛微微乍了起来。「难道就没有人给你们讲过一篇童话吗？俺们畜生个个都有舌头，等到了那地方，俺们就使唤起它来。」

「到了哪儿？」埃德雷德问。被人硬按着去信童话，他心里相当恼火——他从来就不怎么喜欢童话。

===Original===
“Why, in a fairy tale for sure,” said the mole. “Wherever to goodness else on earth do you suppose you be?”

“We’re here,” said Edred, kicking the ground to make it feel more solid and himself more sure of things, “on Arden Knoll.”

“An’ ain’t that in a fairy tale?” demanded the Mouldiwarp triumphantly. “You do talk so free. You called me, and here I be. What d’you want?”

“Are you,” said Elfrida, thrilling with surprise and fear, and pleasure and hope, and wonder, and a few other things which, taken in the lump, are usually called “a thousand conflicting emotions,”—“are you the ‘badge of Arden’s house’?”

“Course I be,” said the mole—“what’s left of it; and never did I think to be called one by the Arden boy and gell as didn’t know their own silly minds. What do you want, eh?”

“We told you in the spell,” said Elfrida.

===Chinese===
「嗨，当然是在童话里喽。」鼹鼠说。「不然你们自个儿想想，天底下还能是哪儿？」

「我们在这儿，」埃德雷德说，用脚蹭了蹭地面，好让脚下觉得更实着些，也让自己对事情更有底，「在阿登丘上。」

「那不就在童话里吗？」白鼹穆迪沃普得意洋洋地质问。「你们说起话来可真轻巧。你们召唤了俺，俺就来了。你们要什么？」

「你是不是，」埃尔弗丽达说，浑身激荡着惊讶与恐惧、欢喜与希望、惊叹，还有另外几样东西——这些凑作一堆，通常就被称作「千百种交战不休的心情」——「你是不是『阿登之家的徽记』？」

「俺当然是。」鼹鼠说——「如今剩下的这一点儿就是。俺做梦也没想到，有朝一日竟被阿登家一个连自个儿的傻心思都拿不稳的小子跟丫头这么唤来唤去。你们要什么，嗯？」

「我们在咒语里告诉过你了。」埃尔弗丽达说。

===Original===
“Oh, be that all?” said the mole bitterly; “nothing else? I’m to make *him* brave and wise and show him de treasure. Milksop!” it said, so suddenly and fiercely that it almost seemed to spit the words in poor Edred’s face.

“I’m not,” said Edred, turning turkey-red. “I got into the house and found the spell, anyway.”

“Yes; and who did all the looking for it? *She* did. Bless you, I was there; I know all about it. If it was showing *her* the treasure, now, there’d be some sense in it.”

“I think you’re very unfair,” said Elfrida, as earnestly as though she had been speaking to a grown-up human being; “if he was brave and wise we shouldn’t want you to make him it.”

“You ain’t got nothing to do with it,” said the mole crossly.

===Chinese===
「嗬，就这个？」鼹鼠辛酸地说；「没别的了？就是要俺把*他*变勇敢变聪明，带他找宝藏。窝囊废！」它说得那么突兀、那么凶，那三个字几乎是啐到可怜的埃德雷德脸上来的。

「我才不是。」埃德雷德说，脸涨得像火鸡红。「不管怎么说，是我进了那座房子，找到了咒语。」

「是啊；可从头到尾找它的是谁？是*她*。老天保佑，俺当时就在场；俺一清二楚。这会儿要是换成带*她*去找宝藏，倒还算有点儿道理。」

「我觉得你太不公平了。」埃尔弗丽达说，那副认真的样子，就像在同一位大人讲话；「他要是已经又勇敢又聪明了，我们也就用不着你把他变成那样了。」

「这事你插不上嘴。」鼹鼠暴躁地说。

===Original===
“Yes, she has,” said Edred. “I mean to share and share with her—whatever I get. And if you could make me wise I’d teach her everything you taught me. But I don’t believe you can. So there!”

“Do you believe I can talk?” the mole asked, and Edred quite definitely and surprisingly said—

“No, I don’t. You’re a dream, that’s all you are,” he said, “and I’m dreaming you.”

“And what do *you* think?” the mole asked Elfrida, who hesitated.

“*I* think,” she said at last, “that it’s getting very dark, and Aunt Edith will be anxious about us; and will you meet us another day? There isn’t time to make us brave and wise tonight.”

===Chinese===
「她插得上。」埃德雷德说。「不管我得着什么，我都打定主意跟她平分。再说，你要真能把我变聪明，你教我的每一样，我都会教给她。可我不信你办得到。就是这么着！」

「你信不信俺会说话？」鼹鼠问。埃德雷德的回答干脆得出奇——

「不信。你是个梦，就这么回事。」他说，「是我正梦着你呢。」

「那*你*怎么想？」鼹鼠问埃尔弗丽达。她犹豫了一下。

「*我*想，」她终于开口，「天越来越黑了，伊迪丝姑姑要惦记我们了；请你改天再见我们，好不好？今晚来不及把我们变勇敢变聪明了。」

===Original===
“That there ain’t, for sure,” said the mole meaningly.

“But you might tell us where the treasure is,” said Edred.

“That comes last, greedy,” said the mole. “I’ve got to make you kind and wise first, and I see I’ve got my work cut out. Good night.”

It began to move away.

“Oh, *don’t* go!” said Elfrida; “we shall never find you again. Oh, don’t! Oh, this is dreadful!”

===Chinese===
「那是当然，今晚确实来不及。」鼹鼠意味深长地说。

「可你可以告诉我们宝藏在哪里呀。」埃德雷德说。

「那是最后一桩，小馋猫。」鼹鼠说。「俺得先把你变善良、变聪明，俺瞧得出来，这活儿够俺受的。晚安。」

它开始走开了。

「噢，*别*走！」埃尔弗丽达说；「我们就再也找不着你了。噢，别走！噢，这可糟透了！」

===Original===
The mole paused.

“I’ve *got* to let you find me again. Don’t upset yourself,” it said bitterly. “When you wants me, come up on to the knoll and say a piece of poetry to call me, and I’ll come,” and it started again.

“But what poetry?” Edred asked.

“Oh, anything. You can pick and choose.”

Edred thought of “The Lays of Ancient Rome.”

===Chinese===
鼹鼠停了下来。

「俺*总得*让你们再找得着俺。别自个儿折磨自个儿了。」它辛酸地说。「你们要找俺，就上丘顶上来，念一首诗唤俺，俺就来。」说完它又动身了。

「可念什么诗呢？」埃德雷德问。

「嗷，什么都行。随你们挑，随你们选。」

埃德雷德想起了《古罗马谣曲》（“The Lays of Ancient Rome”）。

===Original===
“Only ’tain’t no good without you makes it up yourselves,” said the Mouldiwarp.

“Oh!” said the two, much disheartened.

“And course it must be askin’ me to kindly come to you. Get along home.”

“Where are *you* going?” Elfrida asked.

===Chinese===
「只有一条，诗得你们自个儿编的才顶用。」白鼹穆迪沃普说。

「噢！」姐弟俩齐声叫苦，大为泄气。

「还有，诗里当然得求俺赏脸到你们跟前去。回家去吧。」

「*你*去哪儿？」埃尔弗丽达问。

===Original===
“Home too, of course,” it said, and this time it really *did* go.

The two children turned towards the lights of Ardenhurst Station in perfect silence. Only as they reached the place where the down-turf ends and the road begins Edred said, in tones of awe, “I say!”

And Elfrida answered, “Yes—isn’t it?”

Then they walked, still without talking, to the station.

The lights there, and the voices of porters and passengers, the rattle of signal-wires and the “ping, ping” of train signals, had on them the effect of a wet sponge passed over the face of a sleeper by some “already up” person. They seemed to awaken from a dream, and the moment they were in the train, which fortunately came quite soon, they began to talk. They talked without stopping till they got to Cliffville Station, and then they talked all the way home, and by the time they reached the house with the green balconies and the smooth, pale, polished doorknocker they had decided, as children almost always do in cases of magic adventure, that they had better not say anything to anyone. As I am always pointing out, it is extremely difficult to tell your magic experiences to people who not only *will* not, but *cannot* believe you. This is one of the drawbacks of really wonderful happenings.

===Chinese===
「自然是也回家。」它说。这一回它可是*真的*走了。

两个孩子朝着阿登赫斯特车站（Ardenhurst Station）的灯火走去，一路缄默。直到走到丘原草皮尽头、大路开始的地方，埃德雷德才用一种满怀敬畏的声调开口：「我说！」

埃尔弗丽达答道：「是啊——是不是？」

随后他们仍是一言不发，走到了车站。

车站里的灯火、搬运夫和旅客的人声、信号线的哗啦声和火车信号的「叮，叮」声，对他们起的作用，就像某个「早已起床」的人拿一块湿海绵，抹过睡客的脸。他们仿佛从梦中醒了过来，而一旦上了火车——幸而火车来得很快——他们立刻打开了话匣子。他们一路说个不停，说到克利夫维尔车站（Cliffville Station），下了车又一路说到家。等走到那所带绿阳台、门上装着磨得光滑白亮的黄铜门环的房子门口，他们已经拿定了主意——碰上魔法奇遇，孩子们十有八九都是这么办的——最好是任谁也别说。我一向都在指出：要把你的魔法经历讲给那些不但*不肯*信、而且*没法*信的人听，是难上加难的。这正是真正奇妙的事件的一大缺憾。

===Original===
Aunt Edith had not come home, but she came as they were washing their hands and faces for supper. She brought with her presents for Edred’s birthday—nicer presents, and more of them, than he had had for three years.

She bought him a box of wonderfully varied chocolate and a box of tools, a very beautiful bat and a cricket-ball and a set of stumps, and a beetle-backed paintbox in which all the colours were *whole* pans, and not half ones, as they usually are in the boxes you get as presents. In this were beautiful paintbrushes—two camel’s-hair ones and a sable with a point as fine as fine.

“You are a dear, auntie,” he said, with his arms very tight round her waist. He was very happy, and it made him feel more generous than usual. So he said again, “You *are* a dear. And Elfrida can use the paintbox whenever I’m out, and the camel’s-hair brushes. Not the sable, of course.”

===Chinese===
伊迪丝姑姑还没回家，可在他们洗脸洗手准备吃晚饭的时候，她回来了。她带回了给埃德雷德生日的礼物——比他三年来收过的都更精美，也更多。

她给他买了一盒花样繁得出奇的巧克力、一盒工具，一支顶漂亮的球板、一只板球和一副三柱门柱，还有一只甲壳虫背似的拱盖颜料盒，盒里每一格颜料都是*整*块的，不像人家送你的那种盒子里通常只剩半块。盒中还配有漂亮的画笔——两支驼毛的，一支貂毫的，笔尖细得不能再细。

「你真是顶好顶好的姑姑。」他说，双臂紧紧搂住她的腰。他快活极了，快活得比平日慷慨。于是他又说了一遍：「你*就是*顶好顶好的。我不在家的时候，埃尔弗丽达可以用我的颜料盒，还有那两支驼毛笔。貂毫的那支当然不行。」

===Original===
“Oh, Edred, how jolly of you!” said Elfrida, quite touched.

“I’ve got something for Elfrida too,” said Aunt Edith, feeling among the rustling pile of brown paper, and tissue paper, and string, and cardboard, and shavings, that were the husks of Edred’s presents. “Ah, here it is!”

It was a book—a red book with gold pictures on back and cover—and it was called *The Amulet*. So then it was Elfrida’s turn to clasp her aunt round the waist and tell her about her dearness.

“And now to supper,” said the dear. “Roast chicken. And gooseberry pie. And cream.”

To the children, accustomed to the mild uninterestingness of bread and milk for supper, this seemed the crowning wonder of the day. And what a day it had been!

And while they ate the brown chicken, with bread sauce and gravy and stuffing, and the gooseberry pie and cream, the aunt told them of her day.

===Chinese===
「噢，埃德雷德，你真好！」埃尔弗丽达说，感动得很。

「我也有给埃尔弗丽达的东西。」伊迪丝姑姑说，在窸窣作响的一堆牛皮纸、衬纸、细绳、硬纸板和刨花里翻找——那些都是埃德雷德那些礼物的外壳。「啊，在这儿！」

那是一本书——一本红书，书脊和封面上都有烫金的画——书名叫《护身符》（*The Amulet*）。于是这回轮到埃尔弗丽达搂住姑姑的腰，诉说姑姑的好了。

「现在吃晚饭去吧。」亲爱的姑姑说。「烤鸡。还有鹅莓派。还有奶油。」

孩子们的晚饭向来是面包牛奶，温和而无趣，在他们眼里，这便是这一天压轴的奇观了。而这是怎样的一天啊！

他们吃着焦黄喷香的烤鸡，蘸面包酱，浇肉汁，塞着馅料，还有鹅莓派和奶油，姑姑则跟他们讲起她这一天的事。

===Original===
“It really *is* a ship,” she said, “and the best thing it brings is that we shan’t let lodgings any more.”

“Hurrah!” was the natural response.

“And we shall have more money to spend and be more comfortable. And you can go to a really nice school. And where do you think we’re going to live?”

“Not,” said Elfrida, in a whisper—“not at the castle?”

“Why, how did you guess?”

Elfrida looked at Edred. He hastily swallowed a large mouthful of chicken to say, “Auntie, I do hope you won’t mind. We went to Arden today. You said we might go this year.”

===Chinese===
「那*真的*是一条船。」她说，「它带来的顶好的事情是，咱们再也不用出租房间了。」

「好哇！」这是再自然不过的回应。

「咱们会有更多的钱花，日子过得更舒坦。你们也能上一所真正的好学校。你们猜，咱们要搬到哪儿去住？」

「该不会，」埃尔弗丽达悄声说——「该不会是城堡吧？」

「哟，你怎么猜着的？」

埃尔弗丽达望向埃德雷德。他慌忙咽下一大口鸡肉，说道：「姑姑，请你千万别见怪。我们今天到阿登去了。你说过今年我们可以去的。」

===Original===
Then the whole story came out—yes, quite all, up to the saying of the spell.

“And did anything happen?” Aunt Edith asked. The children were thankful to see that she was only interested, and did not seem vexed at what they had done.

“Well,” said Elfrida slowly, “we saw a mole—”

Aunt Edith laughed, and Edred said quickly—

“That’s all the story, auntie. And I *am* Lord Arden, aren’t I?”

===Chinese===
于是全部经过都倒了出来——是的，一字不落，一直讲到念咒语那一步。

「那么，出了什么事吗？」伊迪丝姑姑问。孩子们庆幸地看到，她只是感兴趣，似乎并没有为他们的所作所为生气。

「这个嘛，」埃尔弗丽达慢吞吞地说，「我们看见了一只鼹鼠——」

伊迪丝姑姑笑了起来，埃德雷德赶紧接着说——

「故事就这么些，姑姑。还有，我*就是*阿登勋爵了，对不对？」

===Original===
“Yes,” the aunt answered gravely. “You are Lord Arden.”

“Oh, ripping!” cried Edred, with so joyous a face that his aunt put away a little sermon she had got ready in the train on the duties of the English aristocracy—that would keep, she thought—and turned to say, “No, dear,” to Elfrida’s eager question, “Then I’m Lady Arden, aren’t I?”

“If he’s lord I ought to be lady,” Elfrida said. “It’s not fair.”

“Never mind, old girl,” said Edred kindly. “I’ll *call* you Lady Arden whenever you like.”

===Chinese===
「对。」姑姑郑重地回答。「你是阿登勋爵。」

「噢，棒极了！」埃德雷德喊道，那张脸乐得那么灿烂，姑姑索性把她火车上备好的一篇小布道收了起来——内容是英国贵族的本分——她想，那篇留待日后也不碍事——然后转过身来，对埃尔弗丽达那句急切的问话「那我就是阿登夫人了，对不对？」回了一声：「不是，亲爱的。」

「他若是勋爵，我就该是夫人。」埃尔弗丽达说。「这不公平。」

「别往心里去，丫头。」埃德雷德和气地说。「你高兴的时候，我随时可以*称*你一声阿登夫人。」

===Original===
“How would you like,” asked the aunt, “to go over and live at the castle *now*?”

“Tonight?”

“No, no,” she laughed; “next week. You see, I must try to let this house, and I shall be very busy. Mrs. Honeysett, the old lady who used to keep house for your great-uncle, wrote to the lawyers and asked if we would employ her. I remember her when I was a little girl; she is a dear, and knows heaps of old songs. How would you like to be there with her while I finish up here and get rid of the lodgers? Oh, there’s that bell again! I don’t think we’ll have any bells at the castle, shall we?”

So that was how it was arranged. The aunt stayed at the bow-windowed house to arrange the new furniture—for the house was to be let furnished—and to pack up the beautiful old things that were real Arden things, and the children went in the carrier’s cart, with their clothes and their toys in two black boxes, and in their hearts a world of joyous anticipations.

===Chinese===
「你们愿不愿意，」姑姑问，「*现在*就搬过去住在城堡里？」

「今晚？」

「不，不。」她笑了；「下个星期。你瞧，我得想法把这所房子租出去，会很忙。哈尼塞特太太（Mrs. Honeysett）——从前给你们叔祖管家的那位老太太——写信给律师们，问我们愿不愿意雇她。我还是小姑娘的时候就记得她；她是个可亲的人，会好多老歌。我在这边收尾、打发房客的时候，你们愿不愿意先过去跟她做伴？噢，铃又响了！我看城堡里咱们就不要铃了，好不好？」

事情就这样定了下来。姑姑留在那所带凸窗的房子里布置新家具——因为这所房子要连同家具出租——并把那些真正的阿登家旧物、那些漂亮的物件打包装箱；孩子们则坐着运货马车走了，两人的衣服和玩具装在两口黑箱子里，心里装着满满一世界欢欢喜喜的盼望。

===Original===
Mrs. Honeysett received them with a pretty, old-fashioned curtsey, which melted into an embrace.

“You’re welcome to your home, my lord,” she said, with an arm round each child, “and you too, miss, my dear. Anyone can see you’re Ardens, both two of you. There was always a boy and a girl—a boy and a girl.” She had a sweet, patient face, with large, pale blue eyes that twinkled when she smiled, and she almost always smiled when she looked at the children.

Oh, but it was fine, to unpack one’s own box—to lay out one’s clothes in long, cedarwood drawers, fronted with curved polished mahogany; to draw back the neat muslin blinds from lattice-paned windows that had always been Arden windows; to look out, as so many Ardens must have done, over land that, as far as one could see, had belonged to one’s family in old days. That it no longer belonged hardly mattered at all to the romance of hearts only ten and twelve years old.

Then to go down one’s own shallow, polished stairs (where portraits of old Ardens hung on the wall), and to find the cloth laid for dinner in one’s own wainscoted parlour, laid for two. I think it was nice of Edred to say, the moment Mrs. Honeysett had helped them to toad-in-the-hole and left them to eat it—

===Chinese===
哈尼塞特太太行了一个漂亮的老式屈膝礼迎候他们，那屈膝礼随即化作一个拥抱。

「欢迎回到你们自己的家，少爷。」她说，一手搂着一个孩子，「你也一样，小姐，亲爱的。谁都看得出你们是阿登家的人，你们俩都是。阿登家总是一儿一女——一儿一女。」她有一张温柔而耐心的脸，一双大大的淡蓝色眼睛，笑起来便熠熠闪亮；而她看着孩子们的时候，几乎总是在笑。

噢，那可真美：打开自己的箱子收拾——把自己的衣裳摊进长长的雪松木抽屉里，抽屉外面是弧形打磨光亮的红木面板；把整洁的细布窗帘从格子窗上拉开——那窗子从来就是阿登家的窗子；凭窗远眺，眺过那片一眼望不到头的、旧日里属于自家的土地——从前的阿登家的人，想必也这样眺望过不知多少回。如今它已不再属于自家，这对两颗只有十岁和十二岁的心里的浪漫，几乎算不得一回事。

然后走下自家那道浅浅的、擦得光亮的楼梯（墙上挂着老阿登家众人的画像），发现自家那间镶着木壁板的客厅里，晚餐的桌布已经铺好，摆了两副餐具。哈尼塞特太太给他们布好「洞中蟾蜍」（toad-in-the-hole，面糊烤香肠），转身退下让他们吃的时候，埃德雷德立刻开了口——我认为他这话说得真可爱——

===Original===
“May I pass you some potatoes, Lady Arden?”

Elfrida giggled happily.

The parlour was furnished with the kind of furniture they knew and loved. It had a long, low window that showed the long, narrow garden outside. The walls were panelled with wood, browny-grey under its polish.

“Oh,” said Elfrida, “there must be secret panels here.”

===Chinese===
「阿登夫人，我给您递些土豆好吗？」

埃尔弗丽达快活地咯咯直笑。

客厅里摆的家具是他们熟悉并喜爱的那一路。屋子有一扇长而低的窗，望得见外面狭长的园子。墙上镶着木壁板，在漆光下面呈褐灰色。

「噢，」埃尔弗丽达说，「这儿准有暗板。」

===Original===
And though Edred said, “Secret fiddlesticks!” he in his heart felt that she was right.

After dinner, “May we explore?” Elfrida asked, and Mrs. Honeysett, most charming of women, answered heartily—

“Why not? It’s all his own, bless his dear heart.”

So they explored.

The house was much bigger than they had found it on that wonderful first day when they had acted the part of burglars. There was a door covered with faded green baize. Mrs. Honeysett pointed it out to them with, “Don’t you think this is all: there’s the other house beyond;” and at the other side of that door there was, indeed, the other house.

The house they had already seen was neat, orderly, “bees-whacked,” as Mrs. Honeysett said, till every bit of furniture shone like a mirror or a fond hope. But beyond the baize door there were shadows, there was dust, windows draped in cobwebs, before which hung curtains tattered and faded, drooping from their poles like the old banners that, slowly rotting in great cathedrals, sway in the quiet air where no wind is—stirred, perhaps, by the breath of Fame’s invisible trumpet to the air of old splendours and glories.

===Chinese===
虽然埃德雷德嘴上说「暗个鬼！」，心里却也觉得她说得对。

晚饭后，「我们可以探探险吗？」埃尔弗丽达问。哈尼塞特太太——女人里头顶顶迷人的一位——痛快地答道——

「有什么不可以的？这全是少爷他自己的，上帝保佑他亲爱的小心肝。」

于是他们探了起来。

这座房子比他们头一回扮演窃贼的那个奇妙日子里所见的要大得多。有一扇门蒙着褪了色的绿粗呢。哈尼塞特太太把它指给他们看：「你们可别以为就这么些了：那头还有另一座房子呢。」那扇门的另一侧，果然还有另一座房子。

他们已经见过的这座房子干净、齐整，用哈尼塞特太太的话说，「叫蜜蜂舔过似的」，每一件家具都亮得像一面镜子，也像一桩痴痴的指望。可绿呢门的那一边，却是幽暗，是尘灰，是挂满蛛网的窗户，窗前垂着破烂褪色的帘子，从帘杆上耷拉下来，就像大教堂里那些慢慢朽坏的旌旗，在没有风的静止空气里微微摇荡——兴许，是被名声那支无形号角的气息吹动，吹向旧日荣华与辉光的空气里去吧。

===Original===
The carpets lay in rags on the floors; on the furniture the dust lay thick, and on the boards of corridor and staircase; on the four-post beds in the bedchambers the hangings hung dusty and rusty—the quilts showed the holes eaten by moths and mice. In one room a cradle of carved oak still had a coverlet of tattered silk dragging from it. From the great kitchen-hearth, where no fire had been this very long time, yet where still the ashes of the last fire lay grey and white, a chill air came. The place smelt damp and felt—

“Do you think it’s haunted?” Elfrida asked.

“Rot!” was her brother’s brief reply, and they went on.

They found long, narrow corridors hung crookedly with old, black-framed prints, which drooped cobwebs, like grey-draped crape. They found rooms with floors of grey, uneven oak, and fireplaces in whose grates lay old soot and the broken nests of starlings hatched very long ago.

Edred’s handkerchief—always a rag-of-all-work—rubbed a space in one of the windows, and they looked out over the swelling downs. This part of the house was not built within the castle, that was plain.

When they had opened every door and looked at every roomful of decayed splendour they went out and round. Then they saw that this was a wing built right out of the castle—a wing with squarish windows, with carved drip-stones. All the windows were yellow as parchment, with the inner veil laid on them by Time and the spider. The ivy grew thick round the windows, almost hiding some of them altogether.

===Chinese===
地毯破成一片片摊在地板上；家具上积尘厚厚的，走廊和楼梯的木板上也是；卧房里几张四柱床上，帐幔蒙着尘、发着锈色——被褥上尽是蛾子和老鼠咬出的窟窿。有一个房间里，一只雕花橡木摇篮上还拖着一幅破烂的丝质盖巾。大厨房的壁炉不知多久没生过火了，可上一炉火的灰烬还灰白地堆在那里，一股寒气从中透出。这地方闻着潮，摸着——

「你说这儿闹鬼吗？」埃尔弗丽达问。

「胡扯！」她弟弟简短地答了一句，两人接着往前走。
